Sonova · SOON-CH · the new Memo conveyor's card of 19 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Sonova

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 46,255 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces are favourable now and neutral over three years. The structural floor (4% volume, ageing, under-penetration) is reliable; the 2026 acceleration is partly cyclical replacement timing and a crowded launch window that will annualise in calendar 2027. The single external force that decides Sonova's reported earnings is the Swiss franc, which took all of Sonova's local-currency EPS growth from FY2023 to FY2026 and is guided neutral only for FY2027; a model that assumes no franc drag after FY2027 is assuming a break in a fifteen-year pattern. The competitive picture is intensifying through product, with Demant the strongest rival and the custom category already lost in the VA, but price discipline holds. Amplifon's GN purchase is a sized, slow negative (1% to 2% of sales from FY2028 to FY2030) with an unsized offset in the independent channel. The Consumer should model market growth of 3.5% to 4.5%, price and mix of 0.5 to 1.5 points, a franc drag of 2 to 3 points on core EBIT a year from FY2028, and the Amplifon loss phased roughly one-third each in FY2028, FY2029 and FY2030.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0240C-0558C-0742C-0950C-3570C-3958
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

D4026th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is rising, from moderate to high, in the premium wholesale segment for the next 6 to 12 months, but through product rather than price; the industry's price discipline is intact. Four of the five majors launched premium AI platforms in a single window in August to September 2026: Sonova Phonak EON (new proprietary Hypersonic chip, 25% smaller, 37% better power efficiency, third-generation real-time deep neural network), Demant Oticon Reveal (Dual AI, but on the existing Sirius chip), WS Audiology Signia MaX/Multi-adaptive Xperience (four dedicated deep neural networks) and GN ReSound Sensia (software update on the same chip). Demant's Oticon Zeal custom in-the-ear device took 18% to 24% of the VA custom category within a month of its May 2026 entry and 28% of the VA rechargeable custom category by September 2026, cutting Sonova's custom share there from 61% (combined rechargeable and multi-processor) to 43% to 45%. Demant's wholesale grew 17% organically in Q3 calendar 2026 against Sonova's 11% in H1.

Against that: Sonova's total VA value share was a stable 53.5% in September 2026 (+470 basis points year on year), it has gained wholesale share for eleven consecutive quarters, and channel checks confirm wholesale list prices stable across manufacturers; competitors are introducing lower-tier software options to defend share rather than cutting premium prices. Starkey, GN and Oticon are more promotional with independents than Phonak.

Structural change in intensity: Amplifon's EUR 2.3bn acquisition of GN Hearing (signed March 2026, closing expected late 2026) turns the largest retailer into a manufacturer and raises the stakes in the independent channel (40% to 45% of global distribution), where GN may now be boycotted and the other four makers compete for displaced volume. Demant's KIND acquisition (December 2025) removed Sonova's German retail leadership.

Direction for the next 6 months: harder share contests in independents and the VA, with Demant the strongest competitor (best momentum, highest VA price at USD 692 to 695 against Sonova's USD 540), and with the EON's hardware lead (versus Reveal's and Sensia's software refreshes) the main reason Sonova should hold its own. The risk to watch is ASP (average selling price) dilution through rebates in Europe's cost-sensitive channels, not list-price cuts.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

A584th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: a mild net tailwind for FY2027, turning neutral in FY2028; supplier concentration is a standing tail risk, not a trend.

Tailwinds now: the absence of the FY2026 manufacturing and logistics ramp-up costs (Mexicali regionalisation cost about 80 basis points of gross margin in H1 FY2026) is a large part of H1 FY2027's 230-basis-point gross-margin gain; component cost deflation was noted in FY2026; total material inflow fell industry-wide; procurement refinements are guided to add 2 to 3 points to core EBIT growth (Morgan Stanley); the CHF 50m transformation programme targets CHF 90m of run-rate savings by year four through footprint, automation and value engineering, with net savings from year two (FY2028), back-end loaded. Freight moved from air to sea.

Headwinds: the dual-chip and new-chip platforms cost about 10% more to build than a standard RIC (receiver-in-canal) device (covered by the 10% Sphere price premium); niche medtech buyers get low priority in foundry allocation; memory and advanced packaging shortages are lifting semiconductor prices in 2026 (flagged for IT hardware; relevance to hearing-aid chips is unquantified); Morgan Stanley flagged freight inflation of about 40% and energy prices into 2027; Mexican peso cost inflation as Mexicali scales.

Concentration: balanced-armature receivers come from a Knowles and Sonion near-duopoly that supplies all five majors, so a disruption would hit the whole industry equally rather than Sonova specifically; Sonova monitors 36 critical single-sourced or skin-contact suppliers and has over 95% of direct-material relationships older than five years.

The structural cost lever: the Swiss franc share of the cost base (about 15% of costs against 1% of sales) is to fall below 10% in the mid-term; each 5 points moved out of Switzerland reduces the transaction mismatch materially, which is as much an FX hedge as a cost saving.

For the model: gross margin roughly 74.5% to 75.5% for FY2027 to FY2028 (core basis), with upside to about 76% by FY2029 from footprint savings, offset by retail mix.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C-4216th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the macro force that matters is the Swiss franc, and it bites hardest at the company level, not at the industry level; consumer weakness and the discount rate act at the industry and sector level and matter less.

Currency (company level, decisive). Sonova earns about 1% of sales in francs but carries about 15% of costs in francs; it hedges only 1 to 6 months of transactions and none of its translation exposure (forward contracts CHF 277.7m at March 2026; no hedge accounting). FX translation cut FY2026 reported sales by CHF 221m (6.1 points) and normalised EBITA by CHF 106.5m (150 basis points of margin). Normalised EPS in francs has been flat for four years (CHF 11.14, 10.06, 10.43, 10.42 for FY2023 to FY2026) while local-currency earnings compounded. Sensitivities: 5% on USD/CHF moves sales by CHF 65m and EBITDA by CHF 17m; 5% on EUR/CHF moves sales by CHF 70m and operating profit by CHF 25m; a 5% move in all major currencies moves core operating profit by about 6.5% (calculated by the review). Danish peers report in a euro-pegged krone and hedge up to 18 months (Demant) or 75% to 100% of EBITA for 12 months (GN). For FY2027, FX is now guided neutral (first year in four without a drag), but Maybank expects USD/CHF to slip from 0.815 to 0.80 by mid-2027 and Morgan Stanley sees EUR/CHF moving toward 0.87 by early 2027 (from about 0.93); the Swiss National Bank is on hold and the franc is the risk-off haven in the current regime (the Fitness for Fighting digest's "Swiss stable" reading; Swiss exporters lose 2 to 3 points a year of reported profit).

Consumer (industry level, moderate). Private-pay premium upgrades are discretionary and delayed in inflationary periods; channel checks in mid-2026 describe down-trading. VA (13% of Sonova wholesale) and reimbursed European volumes are insulated.

Discount rate (sector level). European medtech de-rated 2021 to mid-2026 on rates; Sonova has already re-rated (24-month-forward P/E 21.2x, 63rd percentile of five years), so it is more exposed than the sector to a renewed rates squeeze.

For the model: run FY2028 to FY2030 with a base-case franc drag of 1 to 2 points a year on reported sales and 2 to 3 points on core EBIT, not zero as consensus implicitly assumes (see C-67).

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–50

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the material other dynamic is the realignment of the hearing-aid value chain triggered by Amplifon's purchase of GN Hearing and Demant's purchase of KIND; for Sonova it is a known negative (about CHF 50m to 60m of revenue lost from 2027 to 2029) against a less visible positive (independent audiologists punishing GN).

The negative, sized. Amplifon bought about 20% of its hearing aids from Sonova and generated CHF 50m to 60m of Sonova revenue in FY2026 (about 1.5% of group sales, about 3% of wholesale, at Amplifon's low procurement prices of EUR 150 to 200 per unit, so the profit at risk is smaller than the revenue share). Amplifon plans to raise in-house (GN) sourcing from 40% to about 90% by 2029; long-dated supply agreements push most of the shift to late 2028 and 2029 (Morgan Stanley). DNB Carnegie puts the loss at 1% to 2% of group sales over three years; management says the effect is embedded in guidance and downplays FY2027.

The positive, unsized. About 90% of GN Hearing's revenue (sales to Amplifon were about 10%) comes from external customers, mostly independents, VA and Costco, roughly CHF 0.9bn to 1.0bn by my estimate from GN's divested hearing business. Expert calls and Bernstein say independents resent funding a retail competitor and will move volume to non-integrated makers; GN's VA share already fell from 21% to about 9% to 10% over several years, and GN lost Costco app share. If even 5% of GN's external volume moved and Sonova took a third of it, that would be about CHF 15m to 20m a year, partially offsetting the Amplifon loss. This is a hypothesis to research, not a fact.

Demant's KIND. Demant now leads German retail (over 900 clinics, about 12%) against Sonova's GEERS (740 clinics; AudioNova 14% share) and will migrate KIND's purchasing to 95% Oticon within two years; KIND bought some Sonova product, so a small wholesale loss in Germany is likely, and Demant gains scale in lead generation in Sonova's largest market.

Other: Sonova's balance-sheet strength (1.1x) against Amplifon (about 3.0x to 3.3x post-deal) and Demant (3.0x) gives it an uncontested field for retail bolt-ons in the next two years.

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

A702nd of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: entry threats are low in the 18-month horizon and moderate beyond five years; the most credible entrants are adjacent (optical retail), not technology companies.

  • Adjacent entrants (moderate, rising, aimed at retail): optical chains treat audiology as a high-contribution add-on: Fielmann (412 hearing shop-in-shops by end-2024, 20% to 25% audiology EBITDA margins, expanding in Germany, Sonova's largest market), Specsavers (over 1,700 joint clinics, bundling free glasses with premium aids), EssilorLuxottica (18,000 doors, Nuance Audio glasses in 14 countries and 16,000 stores; experts say it has "means, motive and opportunity"). These compete with Sonova's retail and become buyers for its wholesale; Nuance's initial traction is soft. Bernard's Essilor background is relevant here.
  • Standard entrants (very low): a new hearing-aid maker would need chips, software, approvals, service networks and audiologist training; none has appeared; Chinese makers sell in low-end and domestic channels.
  • Orthogonal or innovative entrants (low now, a long-run watch): Apple (FDA-cleared AirPods Pro 2 hearing function), Meta (FDA-approved hearing capability in third-generation glasses), OTC brands. Evidence says they expand awareness and funnel users to clinics; risk rises if regulators loosen professional-fitting requirements or if AI self-fitting matures (Morgan Stanley's long-run concern). Online and tele-audiology remain niche; 60% of audiologists use remote care as a retention tool.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

B588th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are a support today (stable, long relationships; easing component costs) and a low-probability, industry-wide source of risk (receiver duopoly, chip foundry priority).

Sonova designs its own chips (DEEPSONIC, Hypersonic) and algorithms, produces high-tech components in Switzerland, assembles in Vietnam, China and Mexico (Mexicali), and 3D-prints custom shells. It relies on Knowles and Sonion for balanced-armature receivers (Sonion supplies all five majors at 40% to 50% gross margins), external foundries for chips and external batteries (lithium-ion coin cells are about 35% of a premium device's volume, which limits miniaturisation industry-wide). Over 95% of direct-material relationships have lasted over five years; 36 critical suppliers are monitored; over 75 on-site supplier audits; dual sourcing is the stated policy for critical components; inventory buffers (187 days at September 2026) cover launches and trade disruption. Component cost deflation helped FY2026; procurement refinements are guided to contribute 2 to 3 points to core EBIT growth.

Risks: foundry allocation gives niche medtech buyers low priority; semiconductor memory and packaging prices are rising in 2026; a disruption at Sonion or Knowles would hit all makers. The new-chip platforms cost about 10% more per device, offset by premium pricing.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0166C-0364C-0444C-0864C-3264C-3746C-4368
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

C+669th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand has moved from a cyclical trough (2023 to early 2025) back to, and briefly above, its structural trend; the structural floor (about 4% volume growth a year) is highly visible, the cyclical premium above it is not. Volume growth stepped up from soft (2% to 4% value growth assumed at the start of 2026) to about 4% volume in H1 calendar 2026, 3% units in Q2 and about 6% units with about 1 point of price in Q3 calendar 2026 (global value growth 6% to 7% per Demant and Morgan Stanley). Sonova raised its market assumption for FY2027 to 3% to 5% value growth from 2% to 4%. European units ex-UK grew about 6% in Q2; US commercial units +6% in Q3, with managed care still negative; VA purchase values +7% to 8% year on year in August and September 2026.

Why the split matters for the model. The structural layer is demographic and penetration-driven: the over-65 population doubles to 1.55bn by 2050, adoption is about 38% in North American and Western European medical channels (other sources say 10% to 15% of all hearing-impaired), and about 4% in China and India. Long-run volume growth of 4% (DNB Carnegie; 4% to 6% in other sources) plus 0% to 2% price and mix is the reliable base. The cyclical layer is replacement timing: upgrade deferrals affect nearly 50% of industry sales, and the 2026 acceleration is partly a pent-up replacement wave pulled forward by four simultaneous premium AI launches (August 2026) and France's four-year post-reform renewal cycle. Bernstein's base rate is that US downturns rarely last more than two quarters, and recoveries in private channels follow soft phases; but channel checks in July to October 2026 still describe price-sensitive patients down-trading and delaying upgrades on inflation and petrol prices.

Visibility. Two-plus years of visibility exists only for the structural 3% to 5% value growth; the Q3 calendar 2026 rate of 6% to 7% is launch-cycle-driven and should not be extrapolated beyond 12 months (it will annualise in H2 calendar 2027). The monthly VA series and quarterly peer prints (Demant, Amplifon, GN) give good real-time tracking.

For the model: use 3.5% to 4.5% market value growth for FY2027 to FY2028 and 3% to 4% thereafter; treat anything above as share or price, not market.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

A643rd of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the one disruption force that is strengthening, on-device AI sound processing, acts in Sonova's favour; the force that was feared, over-the-counter (OTC) and consumer-electronics substitution, is being disproved; the medium-term watch item is backward integration by the largest retailer, which is a channel disruption rather than a technology one.

AI on the chip, strengthening and favourable. Speech-in-noise is the feature patients value most; real-time deep neural network processing in a hearing aid's power envelope requires proprietary silicon, decade-long R&D cycles and scale. Sonova's DEEPSONIC chip (Infinio Sphere, August 2024; 7.7 billion operations per second) and now the Hypersonic chip (EON, August 2026) give it a two-to-three-year lead in custom silicon according to expert calls, though Bernstein's view is that leads are caught up within one to two cycles. AI raises the R&D cost of competing, which favours the two largest makers (Sonova, Demant at 6% to 9% of sales on R&D) and penalises GN and Starkey. Cochlear Ltd's chief executive has said Cochlear intends to integrate similar neural networks.

OTC and consumer tech, disproved as substitutes. Four years after the US OTC rule (October 2022), OTC penetration is about 1% of the US market and 4% of surveyed adults bought one; return rates near 30%; Sony and Lucid discontinued OTC lines; Sennheiser's own OTC push failed, which is why Sonova is exiting Consumer Hearing. Apple's AirPods Pro hearing-aid function and EssilorLuxottica's Nuance Audio glasses act as awareness funnels that send dissatisfied users to clinics. Morgan Stanley warns that hearing aids could outsource heavy AI computation to smartphones over Bluetooth Low Energy, which would erode the silicon moat in the long run; there is no near-term evidence.

Direction on the company: net positive for 18 to 36 months; the moat is a race (it must be re-won every two years), not a permanent wall.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F4432nd of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: several regulatory changes are under way, mostly mildly negative and slow-acting, with one sudden downside (US Section 232 tariffs) and one long-dated upside tail (US Medicare hearing coverage).

Negatives, slow-acting:

  • US: the One Big Beautiful Bill Act (July 2025) tightens Medicaid eligibility with work requirements phased 2027 to 2029; states may cut optional audiology benefits; managed care channels (Medicare Advantage hearing benefits at 85% to 90% penetration) keep compressing ASPs to USD 300 to 500. Managed care was down mid-to-high single digits in 2026. Sonova's exposure is moderate (managed care is part of US commercial; the US is about 30% of group sales).
  • Germany (Sonova's largest market): about 40% of statutory sickness funds exited automatic six-year replacement agreements, imposing case-by-case approval, a demand bottleneck; statutory reimbursement is fixed at about EUR 800 to 1,000 per device.
  • France: the 100% Santé reform's renewal wave (four-year cycle) supports volume in 2025 to 2026 but with downward pressure on ASPs; it may fade in 2027.
  • China: volume-based procurement (VBP) has hit cochlear implant pricing, a key reason Cochlear Implants fell 11.1% in FY2026 and 10.2% in H1 FY2027.

Sudden downside: a pending US Department of Commerce Section 232 investigation into medical equipment imports could end the hearing-aid industry's historic duty-free status. Sonova supplies the US largely from Vietnam and Switzerland (reciprocal tariff rates cited at 20% and 39%) while ramping Mexicali, Mexico. Manufacturers passed through price increases of over 10% in the 2025 tariff episode, which suggests partial pass-through ability, but the timing gap would hit margins.

Upside tails: US FDA capacity is delaying Integro (cochlear processor) approval, a timing issue only. Expanded adult cochlear candidacy (40% to 60% word recognition) and Medicare's coverage of implants as prosthetics support Advanced Bionics. A press report (14-Nov-26) says the November 2026 US election outcome could reorder federal health policy, and the Democratic platform had proposed adding hearing aids to traditional Medicare; I treat this as a low-probability, high-impact upside for 2028 onward, not a modelling input.

Bearing on the model: a small, steady drag on ASP in public and insured channels (perhaps 0.5 points a year on price), plus a tariff scenario to stress-test.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

C6413th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: two industry drivers are accelerating Sonova's demand now, AI premiumisation of the replacement cycle and the shift of patient preference to rechargeable custom in-the-ear devices; both favour Sonova over the industry, but both are contested by Demant.

AI premiumisation. Patients pay out of pocket for premium tiers where the product is better; the Sector Digest notes that the hearing aid makers' AI chips earned higher prices at launch in 2026. Sonova's Sphere premium is 10% over standard Infinio in the VA, and rechargeable devices reached 69.7% of Sonova BTE and RIC units (66.2% a year earlier). Simultaneous launches by four makers lifted the whole market in Q3 calendar 2026 (6% units), so the driver is accelerating market demand, not only redistributing it.

Custom in-the-ear (ITE) shift. ITE form factors are forecast to grow from 10% of global units to 25% by 2031 (Morgan Stanley); consumer earbuds have reduced stigma; about two-thirds of Zeal users are first-time wearers, which shows the category expands the market. Sonova's Virto R took over 60% of the VA rechargeable ITE category on launch and still holds 43%; Sonova gained about 100 basis points of global ITE share in twelve months and plans an instant-fit custom device within 18 months (DNB Carnegie expects 2027). Demant's Zeal is the stronger premium product here (USD 695, 42% premium to its standard RIC, against Sonova's 10%).

Size of the acceleration: I estimate these two drivers explain 2 to 4 points of Sonova's wholesale growth above market in FY2026 to H1 FY2027, with the Costco and VA channel wins explaining another 2 to 3 points. The AI driver should persist through FY2028 (EON); the custom driver depends on the instant-fit launch.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C+649th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: patient demand supports the thesis now (market value growth back to 3% to 5%, with a launch-driven spike), with moderate fragility concentrated in private-pay premium upgrades.

Supportive. Market volume +4% in H1 calendar 2026 and about +6% in Q3; Europe ex-UK +6%; US commercial +6% in Q3; VA purchases +7% to 8%; premium AI features drive replacement; custom rechargeable ITE attracts first-time users (two-thirds of Zeal users); France's renewal wave; Germany's adoption rate rose from 37% (2018) to 47% (2025) and France's to 55%, creating large replacement pools.

Fragile. Private-pay (about 75% of global retail sales per Amplifon) is discretionary at the margin: channel checks (July to October 2026) describe patients delaying upgrades on inflation and fuel prices and trading down to entry-tier models; US managed care (Medicare Advantage) caps prices and some practices deprioritise managed-care patients; German sickness funds now approve replacements case by case; China consumer weakness hit Asian retail and Demant's Asia growth turned negative.

Customer structure for Sonova (wholesale): independents 43% of wholesale, own retail about 15% of wholesale volume, VA 13% of wholesale (4% to 5% of group), Costco about 4% of wholesale, Amplifon about 3%, managed care and others the rest. No customer above 5% of group revenue, but the VA is a single government buyer on five-year fixed-price contracts, which makes it stable but share-sensitive.

For the model: assume FY2027 to FY2028 market value growth of 4% to 5% easing to 3% to 4% as the launch spike annualises; stress a two-quarter US private-pay stall (Bernstein's base rate) at -2 points of US wholesale growth.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F4629th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators are net mildly hurting through payers and potentially through tariffs, while professional-dispensing rules continue to protect the prescription channel; the position is fragile to two discrete decisions (US tariffs, any loosening of fitting requirements).

Hurting: US Medicaid tightening (2027 to 2029), managed-care reimbursement pressure; German sickness funds' case-by-case approvals and fixed reimbursement (about EUR 800 to 1,000); French price pressure after the 100% Santé reform; China VBP on implants; US FDA capacity delaying Integro; the Section 232 investigation into medical-equipment imports.

Helping: the US OTC rule removed the medical evaluation requirement only for adult mild-to-moderate loss, so custom prescription fitting stays with professionals; EU MDR (2017/745) and FDA class II approvals are entry barriers (MDR transition extended to 2027 and 2028); Medicare covers implants as prosthetics; adult cochlear candidacy is being widened; the VA fully funds veterans' devices; a possible US Medicare hearing benefit is a long-dated upside.

Fragility: a tariff decision could arrive suddenly (Sonova imports US product from Vietnam and Switzerland; Mexicali is the mitigation; the 2025 episode showed over 10% price pass-through industry-wide); a loosening of who may sell hearing aids (Amplifon notes a potential reduction in required qualifications) would favour optical chains and low-price entrants. Everything else is slow and incremental.

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

B688th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the addressable value chain is about USD 33bn (wholesale USD 7.7bn, retail about USD 23bn, implants USD 2bn to 2.5bn), of which Sonova captures about 13%; the market grows about 3% to 5% in value, which allows Sonova 6% to 8% local-currency growth over the mid-term from share, retail consolidation and implant recovery, not the 10% implied by the CHF 6bn ambition without large M&A.

Market sizes and Sonova's share. Wholesale USD 7.7bn (about CHF 6.2bn at 0.80): Sonova wholesale about CHF 1.9bn to 2.0bn, about 27% to 30% (wholesale sales include sales to own retail at transfer prices, so shares are approximate). Retail about USD 23bn: Sonova retail about CHF 1.5bn to 1.6bn, about 7%. Implants USD 2.0bn to 2.5bn: Sonova about CHF 0.25bn, about 15%.

Headroom. Adoption is 38% in developed medical channels and about 4% in China and India; the over-65 population doubles by 2050; independents still hold 40% to 50% of retail value; ITE devices could go from 10% to 25% of units by 2031; adult cochlear candidacy is widening.

Growth arithmetic for the mid-term (my base). Market 3.5% to 4.5% + share gains 0.5 to 1.5 points + bolt-on retail 1 to 1.5 points + implant recovery about 0.3 points in FY2028 to FY2029 - Amplifon phase-out about 0.5 points a year in FY2028 to FY2030 = about 5.5% to 7.5% local currency. Consensus revenue growth of 7.4% (FY2028) and 6.5% (FY2029) in francs, which assume no FX drag, sits at the top of that range.

1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0668C-1057
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

B688th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Sonova is in the early-to-middle phase of a product upcycle that began in August 2024 (Infinio and Sphere) and was renewed in August 2026 (EON); the cycle has not turned down, but its growth rate will step down in H2 FY2027 as three one-off channel tailwinds annualise, before EON's full-year effect supports FY2028.

Where it is: wholesale growth in constant currency went from +9.5% in FY2026 (+10.9% in H2) to +11.0% in H1 FY2027, the largest share gains since the Marvel cycle of 2018. The drivers were Virto R (custom rechargeable, launched November 2025, about CHF 120m to 130m annualised, a 140 to 160 basis-point tailwind to wholesale growth), Infinio Ultra (October 2025), the Costco re-entry with Sennheiser-branded Infinio (Sonite Rise) from March 2026 (Costco about 4% of wholesale; Sonova now above 20% of Costco units), and VA contract gains. EON launched only in late August 2026, so it contributed little to H1.

The turn: Virto R annualises from November 2026, Costco from March 2027, and the VA gains are already in the base (VA share +470 basis points year on year by September 2026). Guidance implies H2 wholesale growth of about 5% (Morgan Stanley). That is the visible deceleration Jefferies' bear case has warned about. Against it, EON is in its first full half, conversion from Infinio was "considerably faster than management's baseline" (Jefferies) with on-time fulfilment above 95%, and inventory days rose to 187 specifically to supply the launch.

My read of the cycle shape for the model: wholesale +11% (H1 FY2027), about +6% to 8% (H2 FY2027, above the guidance-implied 5%), +6% to 8% in FY2028 (EON full year, less Amplifon phase-out starting), then reverting to 4% to 6% in FY2029 before the next platform (two-year cadence points to about August 2028). Retail is a steadier 4% to 6% organic plus 1 to 1.5 points of bolt-ons. Cochlear Implants should trough in H1 FY2027 and recover from H2 FY2027 to FY2028 on Integro (Europe from October 2026; US pending FDA).

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C+5712th of 38

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Taking each thematic driver in turn, and then all together: how strong are the external headwinds and tailwinds, and what is the overall external picture?

Thematic drivers one by one (the cohort's named drivers: market growth, product cycles, pricing, retail clinic expansion).

  • Hearing-aid market growth: tailwind, moderate and strengthening; back to 3% to 5% value growth with a launch-driven spike to 6% to 7% in Q3 calendar 2026. Value-chain level: supportive. Strength: +2 on a -3 to +3 scale.
  • Product cycles: tailwind for Sonova specifically, neutral for the industry; EON is the only new-hardware platform of the four, but the window is crowded and Demant's Zeal has won the custom category. Strength: +1 (company) / 0 (industry).
  • Pricing: tailwind, modest; list prices stable, premium mix rising, price ASP +1% globally, but channel mix (Costco, managed care, VA) caps group ASP. Strength: +1.
  • Retail clinic expansion: neutral to slight headwind; Sonova can buy clinics uncontested, but retail is mid-teens margin, customer acquisition costs are rising, and Demant now leads Germany. Strength: 0 to -1.
  • Vertical integration (Amplifon-GN): headwind to revenue of about 1.5% of sales over three years, partly offset by independents' resentment of GN. Strength: -1.
  • Macro (franc): the largest headwind historically (-150 basis points of margin in FY2026), neutral in FY2027, likely to resume. Strength: -2 over three years, 0 for FY2027.
  • Disruption (AI on chip; OTC): tailwind. Strength: +1.
  • Regulation and tariffs: mild headwind with a tariff tail. Strength: -1.

Overall external picture. For the next 6 to 12 months the external picture is the best it has been for Sonova in three years: market recovered, FX neutral, input costs easing, OTC disproved, and the main competitive threat expressed through product (where Sonova has new hardware) rather than price. Over 18 to 36 months the picture is balanced: the franc is likely to resume taking 2 to 3 points a year off reported EBIT, Amplifon's phase-out lands in FY2028 to FY2030, the launch window's winner will be clear and the market spike will annualise. At value-chain level the headwinds are channel consolidation and payer pressure; at industry level the oligopoly structure and AI barriers are tailwinds; at sector level (European medtech consumables) the market currently pays for earnings upgrades, not for cheapness, which suits Sonova only while upgrades continue.

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Sonova's internal change is real, broad, fast and self-funded: a complete new leadership team narrowed the perimeter, regionalised the organisation, launched a savings programme and set a growth algorithm within a year, and has beaten and raised in its first two prints. The change has not yet produced most of its own financial outputs; the strong H1 FY2027 is mainly the product cycle plus a weak base. The negatives to carry into the model are recurring restructuring exclusions (about CHF 35m to 40m a year), an H2 reinvestment step-up, and capital-allocation discipline that is untested as retail M&A rises and the pay link to ROCE is removed. The most consequential latent decision is what to do with a balance sheet that will fall below 1.0x leverage by FY2028: buybacks (EPS-accretive by 1 to 2 points a year) or larger retail acquisitions (revenue-accretive, margin-dilutive). The Consumer should model the change programme as supporting 50 to 80 basis points of local-currency margin expansion a year from FY2028, and treat the CHF 6bn ambition as requiring unmodelled M&A.

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement42C-1466C-8640C-5954C-8146D-0342
Group judgement

Risks

C-4216th of 38

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Sonova's fundamental risk profile is clean for a medtech: no deceleration, no estimate cuts, low leverage, no accounting red flags. The material risks are sudden and external (the franc, a US tariff), channel-related (Amplifon, independents, a large contract) and product-related (quality, a lost launch window), and they are correlated through the launch window. The dominant risk is price-side: at CHF 290.6 the shares are above the median broker target, at the 80th percentile of their two-year multiple and after a 47% twelve-month rise, so a 20% to 30% fall to a fair trough requires no fundamental damage, only a change in sentiment, and monthly VA data supplies frequent triggers. For the 18-month predictability work, the Consumer should carry a downside case of EPS 10% below base (lost window plus franc) at 16.8x.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

B669th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: standards are improving clearly in operations and cost discipline and modestly in governance; product quality is the one measure that has slipped.

Execution and operations. Mexicali regionalisation ramp costs (80 basis points of gross margin in H1 FY2026) are behind the company and the gross margin rose 230 basis points in H1 FY2027; EON on-time fulfilment above 95% and faster-than-planned conversion from Infinio (Jefferies); inventory built deliberately ahead of the launch (187 days) with management guiding normalisation in H2; the Sonova X business system underpins the programme (85% plausibility rating in the Change Forces report). Days payable fell sharply (94 to 60, March 2025 to March 2026), which management attributes to payment timing.

Cost discipline. Personnel expenses fell in FY2026; Swiss headcount down 8% (1,434 to 1,317 FTEs) while operations FTEs grew 9.5% (moving work to lower-cost sites) and sales, marketing and administration headcount was flat (12,189 against 12,190). A CHF 35m impairment of underperforming IT software was taken openly.

Governance. Fully independent board (eight of eight), a new technology and innovation committee, chair and directors buying shares, shareholding requirements raised to 300% of base fee for the chair and 200% for others, clawback and malus for three years, clean audit opinions (Ernst & Young), immaterial related-party dealings.

Slippage. The hearing-aid reliability rate fell 6.6% year on year in FY2026, missing internal targets; audiologists reported more Phonak repairs, slow repair turnaround and a transactional support culture, and some reduced prescription share. Restructuring and litigation costs recur every year (CHF 44m in FY2025, CHF 40m to 50m guided for FY2026, CHF 35m to 40m for FY2027), which weakens the quality of the core earnings measure (see C-54).

C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

B404th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negatives are individually modest but numerous, and three of them bear directly on earnings quality and the forecast: recurring "one-off" costs excluded from core EBIT, a step-up in reinvestment that caps H2 margins, and weakening capital-allocation incentives as M&A rises.

  1. Recurring exclusions. Restructuring CHF 44m (FY2025), CHF 40m to 50m (FY2026 guide), CHF 35m to 40m (FY2027 guide), plus CHF 28.2m of MED-EL patent settlement costs (FY2026) and a CHF 35m software impairment; about CHF 0.50 a share after tax each year sits outside core EPS. Reported EBITA (CHF 724m) was CHF 87m below normalised (CHF 811m) in FY2026.
  2. Reinvestment step-up. A low double-digit CHF million increase in R&D, IT, digital and operations spending in H2 FY2027, which Morgan Stanley expects to cut H2 margin by about 60 basis points; the mid-term bridge reserves 1 to 3 points of margin for reinvestment.
  3. Capital-allocation incentives. Performance options tied to ROCE are being replaced by PSUs tied to relative TSR from FY2027, just as bolt-on spending rises and no return hurdle is disclosed.
  4. Communication. Removal of mid-term guidance in late 2025; switch of headline metric to core EBIT; the chief executive declines to discuss Costco or margins by customer.
  5. Execution friction. Simultaneous finance, operations and research leadership changes during a regional restructuring; a 6.6% fall in the product reliability rate; complaints about Phonak service from private practices; total employee turnover up to 15.3% from 14.7% (voluntary down to 9.3%).
  6. Working capital. Inventory at 187 days and payables days down from 94 to 60; reversal is guided but not yet shown.
  7. Plausible: a larger retail acquisition at a high multiple in a competitive market such as Germany; a second impairment in the discontinued business before sale.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

C-5418th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: partially congruent and in transition: the old Sonova was engineering-excellent but bureaucratic and centralised (experts say it slowed product development and responsiveness); the new structure aligns goals, organisation and incentives toward commercial execution, but culture lags structure and parts of the customer-facing culture (support, service) are out of line with a premium strategy.

Aligned. Strategy (premium innovation, local execution, operational excellence) matches the organisation (regions owning wholesale and retail; R&D and quality under the chief executive) and the incentives (variable pay on sales growth, EBITA, EPS and free cash flow; sales capped at 250% payout; shareholding requirements raised). Voluntary turnover fell to 9.3% (from 10.5% earlier and 9.7% a year before); D&I index 84%, above medtech peers.

Not yet aligned. (1) Premium positioning against a "transactional, nickel-and-dime" support culture described by some private practices, repair delays and the Costco discount brand that independents dislike. (2) A margin-keeping posture (C-46) in an industry where independents increasingly choose suppliers on relationship and trust. (3) The PSU switch removes ROCE from long-term pay as M&A rises. (4) Total turnover rose to 15.3% with a structural workforce shift; the regional restructure risks local friction.

Comparison with the benchmark firms (Lifco, Avanza, Ryanair). Sonova is not a holistic, system-wide culture business of that kind; it is a well-run industrial-technology company being re-pointed at commercial execution.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

C+4611th of 38

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: six sudden fundamental risks matter; none is likely in any given year, but the franc and a US tariff are the most plausible and largest. Probabilities below are my judgements over 18 months; sizes are first-year core EBIT effects.

  1. Franc spike in a risk-off episode (probability about 15%; size about 8% to 12% of core EBIT). A 10% appreciation within a quarter is about CHF 100m of reported EBITA; hedges cover only 1 to 6 months; the current regime (risk-off, Swiss National Bank on hold, safe-haven flows) makes it plausible.
  2. US Section 232 tariff on hearing aids (probability about 20%; size about 3% to 6% of core EBIT before pass-through). The US is about 30% of sales; supply from Vietnam and Switzerland; Mexicali mitigates; the 2025 episode showed over 10% price pass-through industry-wide, but with a lag.
  3. Product quality failure or recall (probability about 5% to 10%; size 3% to 8% of EBIT plus share loss). Precedent: the 2020 HiRes Ultra cochlear field action (CHF 11m of sales, CHF 37m of costs; provisions still CHF 28m to 37m); the 6.6% reliability-rate fall; early Sphere receiver breakages; a new chip platform carries launch risk.
  4. Loss of a large volume contract (Costco or a VA award) (probability about 5% to 10%; size 2% to 4% of EBIT). Precedent: the non-renewal of a large US contract hit FY2024 EBITA by 8.2% in francs (with other factors); Costco is about 4% of wholesale (about CHF 80m) at a high-20s EBIT margin; the VA is 13% of wholesale.
  5. Value-destroying large acquisition (probability about 15%; size: no EBIT hit but a de-rating and ROCE dilution). CHF 6bn needs CHF 700m to 800m of acquired revenue; a deal above CHF 300m without a hurdle is the alarm.
  6. Component or chip supply disruption (probability under 5%; size a half-year of delayed launches). The receiver duopoly and foundry allocation affect all makers equally.

On the Risks report's "false friend" verdict and 65% joint failure probability. I treat the 65% figure as a method artefact: the competing-hypotheses matrix awards the fewest inconsistencies to a hypothesis about future deterioration that has not yet appeared (the Wisdom Library's "the vaguest explanation wins a fewest-contradictions contest"). Since the August 2026 report, the two dated tests have gone the bull's way (VA share 53.5%, wholesale +11%). The correlation it describes (channel loss plus lost launch window) is real and should be the model's downside scenario.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

F4210th of 12

Layer 3 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading How diffuse or concentrated are the drivers of the difference between the forecasts and the last four to six years?

Judgement: concentrated, in four senses. By cause: two ended headwinds, the franc and the tax rate, explain about two-thirds of the swing from zero EPS growth to about 8% a year, and only the franc is still open. By segment: about three-quarters of profit and nearly all of the above-market growth come from Hearing Aid Wholesale. By product and channel: four discrete wins produced the above-market growth and all four annualise by March 2027. By time: almost every adverse item lands in one reporting period, the first half of FY2028. What is diffuse is the floor underneath: demand, geography and customers.

By cause. Core EPS was flat from FY2023 to FY2026 (CHF 11.14, 10.06, 10.43, 10.42) and I forecast about 8.3% a year to FY2031. Of that swing, about 3.5 points a year is the franc drag shrinking from about five points of EBIT a year to 1.6; about 2 points is the tax rate no longer rising (8% to 16% between FY2023 and FY2026); about 2 points is margin (one from the FY2027 reset, one from later gains); about 0.8 is buybacks. Local-currency growth is not part of the difference: it was there all along. So about 40% of the difference is one external variable, and each further point of annual franc appreciation removes about 1.6% of EBIT and EPS in that year.

By segment and product. On my estimates wholesale earns about CHF 625m of FY2027 core EBIT of CHF 864m (over 70%), retail about CHF 223m and Cochlear Implants about CHF 16m. Wholesale grew 11% in the first half of FY2027 against a market near 5%; the excess came from Costco (new from March 2026, about four points of wholesale growth), Virto R (about 1.5 points), VA share (+470 basis points of value share) and Infinio. Excluding Costco and Virto R, wholesale grew at about the market rate. From April 2027 growth above the market depends on one platform (EON) in one contested channel (independents, 43% of wholesale), where no regular data exist.

By time: the first half of FY2028 (April to September 2027, reported November 2027). In that half: the comparison is the hardest of the cycle (wholesale +11%); Costco and Virto R are fully in the base; Amplifon's switch to GN is under way; the second-half FY2027 investment step-up is in costs with no equivalent a year earlier; the financial result returns to normal after a first half of FY2027 that was nearly free of financial costs (CHF -1.7m); the tax safe harbour has expired; published currency forecasts put the franc at its strongest (Morgan Stanley: EUR/CHF 0.89 by mid-2027 from about 0.93); and a new presentation standard (IFRS 18, from 1 April 2027) moves currency gains and losses into operating profit. On my base that half shows revenue +5%, core EBIT +6% and core EPS about +2%, after +36% a year earlier.

What is diffuse. No customer above 5% of group sales, more than 100 countries, about 3,600 clinics, three-quarters of retail sales private-pay, and a demographic floor of about 4% volume growth. The level of earnings is broadly based; the change in earnings is narrowly based.

Internal corporate change forces

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

A722nd of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the change is complete and recent: in fourteen months Sonova replaced its chair, chief executive, finance chief, operations chief and research chief and refreshed three board seats. The signal is a deliberate shift from an engineering-led, centralised, cost-cutting culture to a commercially led, regional, growth-and-execution culture, staffed largely with people who know the industry from a competitor.

Who and when. Chair Gilbert Achermann (ex-Straumann chief executive and finance chief) from the June 2025 AGM; board cut from ten to eight. Chief executive Eric Bernard from 15 September 2025 (25 years at Essilor including nine on its executive committee; chief executive of WS Audiology 2019 to 2024, which merged Widex and Sivantos); he succeeded Arnd Kaldowski (personal family health reasons). Finance chief Elodie Carr-Cingari from 1 July 2025 (finance chief of Landis+Gyr 2020 to 2025). Chief research officer Anders Rosengren from 1 October 2025. Chief operations officer Roberto di Fiore from 4 December 2025 (ex-WS Audiology global operations). New board members Ingrid Cotoros, Malina Man Lin Ngai and Hooi Ling Tan elected June 2026 (advanced technology, retail and Asia); the two longest-serving directors left.

What it signals. (1) The board wanted commercial execution, especially in retail and Asia; Bernard's Essilor background is multi-brand consumer-health retail. (2) Speed: the regional model was announced two months after he started and the Consumer Hearing exit within nine months. (3) Insider alignment: the chair increased his holding 130% (to 22,520 shares) and every board member bought or held; Bernard bought 4,685 shares. (4) Competitor intelligence: two of the top team ran WS Audiology, the number two maker.

The caveats. Simultaneous replacement of the finance and operations chiefs during a regional restructuring is a coordination risk (named by the Leadership report as the single largest credibility risk). Bernard's WS Audiology record is mixed: the integration was slow and gross margin fell from 59.8% to 56.8% on retail acquisitions, which he attributes to founding-family intervention. The new team's own outputs (savings, regional synergies, Asia) are mostly still forecasts; the FY2026 and H1 FY2027 numbers are largely the product cycle launched in August 2024 under the previous team.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

B+704th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: strategy changed materially in March 2026 ("Leadership Expanded") and is coherent, clearly articulated in pillars and numbers, and resourced from internal cash; its weakest part is the dependence of the CHF 6bn ambition on unannounced acquisitions.

What changed. (1) Perimeter: exit Consumer Hearing (about 5% of revenue, a CHF 35.8m normalised EBITA loss in FY2026, about 200 basis points dilutive). (2) Organisation: from function-specific business units (Hearing Instruments, Audiological Care) to four regions (North America; EMEA and Latin America; Asia-Pacific ex-China; China), each leading wholesale and retail, reporting to the chief executive from 1 April 2026; R&D and quality report directly to the chief executive; hearing-aid and cochlear R&D unified. (3) Growth algorithm: market 3% to 5%, share gains 1 to 3 points, bolt-on M&A 1 to 2 points, giving 5% to 10% local-currency sales CAGR and 7% to 12% core EBIT CAGR; CHF 6bn revenue by FY2030/31. (4) Operations: a CHF 50m programme over three years for CHF 90m run-rate savings by year four; Swiss franc cost share from 15% to below 10%. (5) Asia: a Singapore Asia-Pacific hub and "made for Asia" simpler, cheaper products. (6) Capital: CHF 80m to 100m a year of retail bolt-ons; buybacks suspended for FY2027.

Articulation. Three pillars (innovating for adoption, succeeding locally, excelling in operations), a margin bridge (+2 to 3 points footprint, +1 to 2 points operating leverage, minus 1 to 3 points reinvestment) and a numerical algorithm. That is better than most medtech peers.

Resourcing. Internal cash (operating free cash flow above CHF 500m a year, leverage 1.1x) comfortably funds the programme, the bolt-ons and the dividend. The weakness: CHF 6bn from CHF 3.6bn in five years needs a CAGR of about 10.7%; at the 7.5% midpoint organically-plus-bolt-ons the company reaches about CHF 5.2bn, so CHF 700m to 800m of revenue must come from larger, unannounced acquisitions (management calls it an ambition, not a target). Morgan Stanley calls it fairly ambitious; Deutsche Bank says it cannot be given full credit.

For the model: use the algorithm's middle (6.5% to 7.5% local currency) and exclude larger M&A from the base case.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

B+665th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets been set or raised?

Judgement: near-term guidance has been raised once (November 2026), with credible room for a second raise or a beat; the mid-term framework was reset in March 2026, widened rather than raised, and the reset followed the withdrawal of the previous mid-term guidance in late 2025.

Near-term. FY2027 guidance set in May 2026 at sales +5% to 8% local currency and core EBIT +7% to 10% cc, with FX a drag of 1 to 2 points on sales and 2 to 3 on core EBIT. On 12 November 2026 raised to sales +6% to 8% and core EBIT +10% to 13%, with FX now broadly neutral. In francs the swing is larger: the May guidance implied reported core EBIT growth of about 4% to 8%; the November guidance implies about 10% to 13%. Morgan Stanley calls the raised guidance conservative and the upper end highly likely; the H1 core EBIT growth of 22.9% means H2 needs only about 1% to 6%.

Mid-term. March 2026: sales CAGR 5% to 10% local currency (previously 6% to 9%), core EBIT CAGR 7% to 12% (previously adjusted EBITA 7% to 11%), CHF 6bn by FY2030/31, market assumption lowered to 3% to 5% from 4% to 6%. The floor was cut and the ceiling raised: a widening that protects the floor and signals ambition. Management clarified that 7% to 12% is a multi-year CAGR, not an annual minimum. The previous mid-term guidance had been withdrawn in late 2025, which investors called a communication misstep.

Signal. A new chief executive rebased the mid-term floor and set a cautious first-year guide (FY2027 at 5% to 8% with a 2% to 4% market assumption), then beat it. That is the pattern of a team that prefers to under-promise; two consecutive beats (May 2026 H2 margin 25.4% against 24.1% consensus; November 2026 core EBIT +10% against consensus) support it.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

A723rd of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition?

Judgement: a decisive, value-accretive reduction: Sonova is exiting the only business that did not fit (Consumer Hearing), and has kept Cochlear Implants after review; no further exits are signalled.

The exit. Sennheiser Consumer Hearing was bought in 2022 for an OTC hearing-aid strategy that the market never delivered. It was about 5% of group revenue (H1 FY2027 discontinued sales CHF 114.8m), lost CHF 35.8m at normalised EBITA in FY2026 and CHF 106.5m after tax including impairments; H1 FY2027 loss after tax CHF 56.8m including a CHF 68.3m pre-tax impairment. It was classified as discontinued from March 2026 and completion is expected within FY2027 (rated above 90% likely). Effect: about 200 basis points on the continuing margin (Deutsche Bank) or about 100 basis points by FY2028 (Rothschild & Co Redburn, measuring differently), 578 FTEs and a non-core retail culture removed, simpler equity story. Sale proceeds are not disclosed; I would assume they are small.

What stays. Cochlear Implants (Advanced Bionics, about 7% of sales, 15% to 16% global share behind Cochlear Ltd's 60% to 65%, margin about 6.8% in FY2026 versus 11.4% historically) was reviewed and kept; management is unifying its R&D with hearing aids. Retail stays and grows. Sonova still operates in over 100 countries.

For the model: continuing operations only; no proceeds assumed; the 200 basis points is already in the restated FY2026 base (the comparatives are restated), so it is not an additional uplift to FY2027 margin. That last point matters: some sell-side commentary reads the exit as a forward margin tailwind, but on a continuing-operations basis it is already in the numbers, except for any stranded costs being removed.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

D5822nd of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: entry is incremental and adjacent, which is sensible: deeper Asia, mobile hearing care, rechargeable custom devices, and digital tinnitus; none is a distant diversification. The margin risk lies in Asia and retail, not in the choice of markets.

Asia. Asia-Pacific grew 11.4% cc in H1 FY2027, with China and Japan wholesale both up mid-teens (Japan close to 20%). A Singapore regional hub (with the Economic Development Board) will develop "made for Asia" simpler, cheaper devices; penetration in China and India is about 4%. Risk: lower ASPs and margins (Deutsche Bank, Jefferies); management aims to replicate European share levels over the mid-term. The Chinese cochlear business (about 8% of Advanced Bionics sales) remains under VBP pressure.

Products. Rechargeable custom ITE (Virto R; an instant-fit version within 18 months), EON (new chip), Infinio Ultra with EasyGuard wax protection (up to 38% fewer service appointments), the Roger accessory ecosystem (schools and paediatrics), SilentCloud digital tinnitus app (bought December 2025; 740 million sufferers), a Spiral Therapeutics R&D partnership and equity stake (April 2026) in inner-ear therapeutics.

Channels. Costco re-entry with Sennheiser-branded devices (low ASP, high-20s EBIT margin because of low cost to serve), mobile hearing care (Høreservice Danmark), US clinics (Harbor Audiology), 60 retail points of sale acquired in seven months.

Sensibleness. All are close to the core (same customers, channels or technology), which the Wisdom Library's core-versus-adjacency model rates as the high-success zone. The one item to watch is Costco: it lifts volume and EBIT but dilutes group ASP and irritates independent audiologists who compete on price.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

B+627th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms, and are the rises real, nominal or latent?

Judgement: real but modest price gains, delivered mostly through mix (premium platforms and rechargeables) rather than like-for-like list increases; the evidence is applied, not conjectural; group ASP is held back by channel mix.

Evidence of real price. Sphere commands a 10% premium over standard Infinio, defended in the VA; EON is positioned as a premium platform; retail delivered "positive average selling price developments" in H1 FY2027; global hearing-aid ASPs rose about 1% in Q2 calendar 2026 on mix; Amplifon's report says manufacturers passed through price increases of over 10% in 2025 to offset tariffs and input inflation, with Phonak raising wholesale prices aggressively; an industry expert says Sonova has used its number-one position to raise prices through cycles; Sonova refuses to negotiate wholesale prices with independents and is the least promotional maker. Swiss CPI is 0.6% and input costs are easing, so any price above 1% is real.

What limits it. Channel mix: the fastest-growing channels are low-priced (Costco and managed care at USD 300 to 500, VA at USD 480 to 540) against independents at USD 600 to 1,100; Costco volume therefore lowers group ASP while raising profit. Sonova's VA ASP (USD 540) trails Demant's (USD 692) because Demant's Zeal sits only in the top price tier at a 42% premium, against Sonova's 10%; Sonova has chosen volume share in the VA over price. French reform pushes ASPs down; German statutory reimbursement is fixed.

Classification: real and applied at the product and premium-tier level (about 1 to 2 points a year of price and mix in wholesale); nominal to slightly negative at the group average when channel mix is included. Latent: if EON proves superior, management could follow Demant's top-tier pricing with an instant-fit custom device.

For the model: wholesale price and mix +1 to 2 points a year; retail ASP +1 point; group ASP effect after channel mix about +0.5 to 1 point.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

F4831st of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: M&A is being stepped up, from CHF 45m to 77m a year in FY2025 to FY2026 to a declared CHF 80m to 100m a year of retail bolt-ons, with larger deals implied by the CHF 6bn ambition; strategically sensible (density in existing retail markets while rivals are financially constrained), but the discipline is unproven because no return hurdle or post-deal review is disclosed.

Pace. Acquisitions (net assets acquired) were CHF 594m in FY2022, 261m in FY2023, 102m in FY2024, 77m in FY2025, 45.5m in FY2026 and 20.7m in H1 FY2027 (about CHF 50m for 60 points of sale in the first seven months of FY2027). The FY2027 run-rate is therefore near the CHF 80m to 100m budget. Bolt-ons added 1.3 points to retail growth in H1 (about 0.5 points to group growth). Reaching CHF 6bn needs CHF 700m to 800m of revenue from larger acquisitions in "selected strategic countries" (Morgan Stanley), which at typical retail multiples (1.0x to 2.0x sales) would cost roughly CHF 0.7bn to 1.6bn by my estimate.

Strategic sense. Demant (3.0x) and Amplifon (about 3.3x post-GN) are constrained, so Sonova faces little competition for clinics. Owned retail secures about 15% of Sonova's own wholesale volume and feeds R&D with fitting data. Local density lowers lead-generation costs.

Discipline and integration. Retail earns mid-teens margins against wholesale above 30%, so every retail deal dilutes group margin and probably return on capital (Rothschild & Co Redburn's central caution; Redburn Atlantic shows retail ROIC suppression). Bernard says "we are extremely careful about the management of the ROCE", but the Leadership report finds no disclosed hurdle or post-acquisition review; Amplifon by contrast applies a hurdle above 12%. History: Alpaca Audiology (December 2021) took about twelve months to integrate; Sennheiser Consumer (2022) failed. The long-term incentive moves from ROCE-linked options to relative-TSR PSUs from FY2027, which removes the direct pay link to return on capital just as M&A rises.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

D5222nd of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: currently the opposite: buybacks are suspended, the CHF 1.5bn programme is paused, and leverage is falling below the bottom of the 1.0x to 1.5x corridor; the balance sheet is building capacity that management will have to deploy by FY2028, and brokers are mooting a buyback restart.

Facts. Net debt CHF 1,072m at end-September 2026, 1.1x (FactSet 1.01x); target corridor 1.0x to 1.5x. Dividend CHF 4.70 (+7%; about 40% to 45% payout; CHF 279.8m paid June 2026). No buybacks in FY2026 or FY2027; buybacks of CHF 732m (FY2022) and 486m (FY2023) preceded the pause. FactSet consensus free cash flow is CHF 684m in FY2027 and 773m in FY2028.

The arithmetic (my estimate). Free cash flow of about CHF 600m to 700m a year, less dividends of about CHF 290m to 320m and bolt-ons of CHF 90m, leaves about CHF 200m to 300m a year to reduce debt. Absent larger deals or buybacks, leverage would fall to about 0.6x to 0.8x by March 2028. To stay at 1.0x to 1.5x, Sonova must deploy CHF 0.5bn to 1.0bn by FY2028 through larger M&A, a buyback, or both.

Who is mooting what. Morgan Stanley and Bernstein say buybacks may resume once leverage targets are consistently met; Deutsche Bank sees Demant's buyback returning mid-2027; Demant already announced a DKK 3bn programme (October 2026), and GN plans DKK 5bn. Bernard says strong cash conversion gives "ample firing power" for retail acquisitions without stretching leverage, which points the cash at M&A first.

For the model: base case no buyback in FY2027; a buyback restart (CHF 200m to 400m a year) from FY2028 is a 40% to 50% probability by my judgement and is worth 1 to 2 points of EPS growth a year; a large retail acquisition is the alternative use and would add revenue but dilute margin.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–64

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: four further positives are present and material for the model: the Swiss franc cost base relocation, the hearing-aid and cochlear R&D unification feeding the Integro recovery, the regional model's commercial effect, and the integrated wholesale-retail feedback loop.

  1. Franc cost relocation (structural, under way). Moving the franc cost share from about 15% to below 10% (international procurement hubs, Mexicali, Vietnam, Singapore) narrows the transaction mismatch; each 5 points moved reduces the margin sensitivity to a franc move by about one-third. This is the only lasting fix for Sonova's biggest earnings volatility and is unique to the new team's agenda.
  2. Cochlear recovery (plausible, H2 FY2027 to FY2028). Integro (sound localisation, direct Roger access, Roger On Sphere link) launched in Europe in October 2026; US approval is pending on FDA capacity. Management says patients deferred upgrades ahead of the launch, so demand is delayed rather than lost. Full commercialisation adds about 1 point to group growth; segment margin back from 6.8% toward 11.4% adds roughly 30 basis points to group margin (my estimate).
  3. Regional model (present, effects forecast). Four regional heads own wholesale and retail; Morgan Stanley expects closer alignment to amplify launches; Asia (+11.4% cc) is the early evidence.
  4. Integrated feedback loop (present). Owned retail (39% of revenue, about 3,600 clinics) gathers fitting data for R&D and secures shelf space; the Lead Generation Factory produced over 600,000 qualified leads in FY2026; flagship World of Hearing stores generate twice average store sales.
  5. Insider alignment (present). Chair and board net buying; tighter shareholding requirements.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

D5019th of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: no controlling actor is pushing for change; the register is stable and passive, so strategy is set by the board and management alone, and the Consumer should not expect an external catalyst.

Sonova has a single class of 59,626,809 registered shares, a 10% voting-rights cap (no exceptions granted in the period), an 82.1% free float, and no shareholders' agreement. The largest holders are Beda Diethelm (11.26%) and the Hans-Ulrich Rihs family (6.18%), descendants of founding-era owners, neither of whom has signalled a strategic view in the sources. No activist campaigns or short-seller reports have ever been published on Sonova. Short interest was high and volatile (above 19% of free float in late 2025 per Bernstein), but that is trading positioning, not governance pressure. The 10% voting cap protects management from a hostile approach; the counterpart is that it would shield underperforming management, though the board has just replaced the whole team itself.

Contrast with peers: Demant is controlled by the William Demant Foundation (about 59%), Amplifon by the Holland family (68.48% of votes), so Sonova is the most market-exposed of the European hearing groups, which raises the board's accountability but removes the possibility of a controlling holder forcing a sale or break-up.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

A683rd of 38

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: strong, broad and well resourced, among the most comprehensive corporate change programmes in European medtech in 2025 to 2026; the remaining question is maturity, because most of the change's own financial outputs are still forecasts.

Breadth. Leadership (all five top roles and the chair), perimeter (Consumer exit), organisation (four regions; R&D and quality under the chief executive), operations (CHF 90m programme; franc cost relocation), capital (bolt-on machine; latent buyback capacity), product (EON, Virto R, Integro, instant-fit pipeline) and incentives (higher shareholding requirements). Five of the twelve internal forces are strong (memo, October 2026); none of the seven red flags present.

Resourcing. Self-funded: free cash flow of about CHF 600m to 700m a year, leverage 1.1x, CHF 50m programme cost against CHF 90m savings, R&D at about 6% of sales maintained.

Strength of the evidence. H1 FY2027 is the first half in which part of the change shows in the numbers: core EBIT +22.9% cc, gross margin +230 basis points, free cash flow CHF 266m against CHF 60m, Asia +11.4%. But I attribute the bulk of H1's growth to the product cycle launched under the previous team (Infinio, Virto R, Costco, VA) and to the absence of FY2026's ramp costs; the new team's own outputs (net savings from FY2028, regional synergies, Asia build, franc relocation) are ahead.

For the model: the change programme supports core EBIT margin rising roughly 50 to 80 basis points a year from FY2028 to FY2030 in local currency (savings plus operating leverage less reinvestment and retail mix), with franc translation the main offset. I would not model the top of the 7% to 12% core EBIT CAGR.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

C6011th of 38

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: a reasonable staircase with one strong step in each period (the EON launch window's VA evidence, the FY2028 guide and capital-allocation decision, and the next platform), but most steps are continuations of a known story rather than novel; the most novel potential steps (a buyback restart, an instant-fit custom device, an Integro US approval) are the least dated.

Next 6 months (to May 2027).

  • Monthly VA data for October to March (each about a month after the month): the first full months of EON, including the smaller Sphere form factor aimed at the November VA procurement window. Novelty medium; well-founded (EON shipping, VA share 53.5%).
  • Demant's Capital Markets Day (24 November 2026): read-across on industry growth and Demant's targets; a cut to Demant's mid-term range could be read either as industry caution or as Sonova's relative strength. Novelty low for Sonova.
  • Completion of the Consumer Hearing sale (within FY2027). Novelty low; well-founded (final stages).
  • Integro: wider European availability through calendar 2027; US FDA approval timing uncertain. Novelty medium; moderately founded (regulatory).
  • Amplifon-GN closing (late 2026) and first evidence of independents' response. Novelty medium; uncertain direction.
  • FY2027 results on 18 May 2027: likely at or above the top of guidance (sales about 8%, core EBIT about 13%), FY2028 guidance, first update on the CHF 90m programme, and possibly a capital-allocation statement. Novelty medium; well-founded.

Months 6 to 18 (June 2027 to May 2028).

  • A buyback restart or a larger acquisition as leverage falls below 1.0x. Novelty high; moderately founded (arithmetic forces a choice; management favours M&A).
  • An instant-fit rechargeable custom device to counter Zeal ("within 18 months"; DNB Carnegie expects 2027). Novelty high; moderately founded (no firm date).
  • Integro US launch and cochlear growth back to positive. Novelty medium.
  • First net savings from the programme (FY2028) and visible franc cost relocation. Novelty medium; well-founded.
  • H1 FY2028 results (November 2027), when EON's annualisation and Amplifon's first phase-out effect show. Mixed.

Months 18 to 36 (June 2028 to November 2029).

  • The next premium platform (two-year cadence from August 2024 and August 2026 points to about August 2028). Novelty high; founded on cadence only.
  • CHF 90m savings at full run-rate (about FY2030), franc cost share below 10%, Asia scale. Novelty medium.
  • Progress toward CHF 6bn (needs acquisitions). Novelty uncertain.
  • Possible US Medicare hearing coverage (a low-probability policy upside).

Negatives on the same staircase: H2 FY2027 deceleration, Amplifon's phase-out from 2027, a resumption of franc drag, Demant's next platform (about August 2027 if on its cycle).

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

B646th of 38

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

B646th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure is one of the strongest in medtech: five makers hold over 85% to 94% of wholesale, compete through two-year platform cycles rather than price, kept list prices stable through the 2023 to 2025 slowdown, and have kept OTC and consumer electronics at about 1% share. Sonova is number one in wholesale, among the two most advantaged makers in AI silicon and channel breadth, and has gained share for eleven quarters. The support is fragile in two places: the technology lead rotates every two years and four platforms launched at once in August 2026 (Demant's Zeal has already taken the VA custom category), and the downstream channel is consolidating and integrating (Amplifon-GN, Demant-KIND, Costco, optical chains), which costs Sonova about CHF 50m to 60m of Amplifon revenue and some German volume while possibly gifting it GN's independent customers. Historical precedent says hearing-aid shares outperform when a product cycle is won from a low multiple; Sonova is at the stage where the cycle is being won and the multiple has already re-rated. For the model: price and mix positive (+0.5 to 1.5 points a year), share gains fading from 1 to 2 points to 0 to 1 point by FY2029, channel losses phased FY2028 to FY2030.
