C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?C+649th of 38full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C-5618th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market is a net help today (Sonova sells through every channel and owns 39% of its revenue at the retail stage), but it is the most fragile part of the value chain because intermediaries are consolidating and integrating.

Helping. Owned retail (about 3,600 clinics) secures volume, data and price realisation; the VA (five-year fixed-price contracts, audiologists familiar with Phonak software) is a 53.5% share position; Costco re-entry adds high-volume, high-20s-EBIT-margin business; independents value Phonak's fitting software, Roger compatibility (schools and paediatrics) and premium technology; Sonova's balance sheet lets it buy clinics while rivals cannot.

Hindering. (1) Amplifon (CHF 50m to 60m) is leaving as a customer, 2027 to 2029. (2) Demant's KIND will convert over 95% of KIND's purchases to Oticon within two years. (3) Independents complain about Phonak's transactional support culture, repair delays and the cheaper Sennheiser brand at Costco undercutting their prices; some redirect prescriptions, and Starkey is praised for service and price. (4) Customer acquisition and lead-generation costs are rising across Sonova's retail network (management acknowledged it; Jefferies flagged it), and retail margins are mid-teens. (5) Managed care middlemen depress clinic economics.

Fragility. Moderate to high over three years: the independent channel (the highest-margin wholesale channel, USD 600 to 1,100 per device) is where integration conflict plays out, and Sonova is both a retailer and a supplier to independents. The early-warning indicators are VA monthly share, independent clinic share in channel checks, and retail sales and marketing as a share of retail sales.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F4629th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors are behaving aggressively on product and rationally on price; net, they hurt Sonova modestly in the next 6 to 12 months (Demant above all), and Sonova's position is robust in total share but fragile in specific categories (custom ITE) and channels (independents in Europe).

  • Demant: the strongest competitor today: group organic growth 11% and wholesale 17% in Q3 calendar 2026; Zeal (custom, USD 695, 28% of VA rechargeable custom by September 2026) plus Reveal (Dual AI, launched August 2026 on the existing Sirius chip; some clinicians see limited improvement over Intent); KIND conversion; balance sheet repairing; buyback resumed; Capital Markets Day 24 November 2026 may cut its mid-term target to 5% to 7% from 6% to 8%. Hurts Sonova in VA custom, German retail and independents.
  • WS Audiology: Signia MaX with four neural networks (strong technical story); Signia Active Mini IX in custom rechargeable (May 2026); private, Bernard's former company. Neutral to slight hurt.
  • GN (ReSound): Sensia (software refresh), 5% organic growth in Q3 2026; distracted by its sale to Amplifon; losing VA and Costco share. Helps Sonova (share donor).
  • Starkey: gaining in some private clinics on Omega AI, service and price; lost VA share to 9.6%. Mild hurt in US independents.
  • Amplifon-GN: a retailer becoming a competitor; hurts via the phase-out.

Fragility. Total VA share is stable at 53% to 54%; wholesale share gains continued in H1 FY2027; EON is the only new-chip platform of the four. But Sonova has already lost the VA custom category lead, and its share gains are concentrated in channels (VA, Costco) that were one-off wins.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F4629th of 38full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

B586th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption is acting on the downstream channel (retail consolidation and backward integration) and on the technology frontier (AI silicon), much less on demand or suppliers; it leaves Sonova's wholesale franchise robust in technology and moderately fragile in channel access.

  • Demand (patients): AI features and rechargeable custom devices pull patients into premium upgrades and attract first-time users; consumer earbuds reduce stigma. Favourable.
  • Competition (makers): AI silicon raises R&D and scale requirements, pushing share to Sonova and Demant and away from GN and Starkey; but simultaneous launches shorten leads. Favourable but contested.
  • Channel (retailers, buying groups, payers): the strongest disruption: Amplifon becoming a maker; Demant integrating KIND; optical chains entering; Costco growing; managed care squeezing clinic economics; independents resisting integrated suppliers. Mixed to negative for Sonova.
  • Regulators and politics: OTC rule (failed to disrupt); possible tariffs; payer pressure. Mildly negative.
  • Suppliers: chip foundry priority for consumer electronics; receiver duopoly; the possibility that AI processing moves to the phone (Morgan Stanley), which would shift value toward smartphone ecosystems. A long-run risk.

Fragility: the company's position would be most damaged by a combination of channel loss (Amplifon, KIND, independents' backlash) and a lost platform cycle at the same time; the Risks report put the joint probability of compounding channel and competitive failure at 65% (a high figure I regard as a method artefact of the "false friend" analysis; see C-81), but the correlation itself is real.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B+804th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Where does the company rank by market share or size, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement: Sonova is number one in hearing-aid wholesale, number two in hearing-care retail and number three in cochlear implants; the wholesale lead is real but narrow (2 to 4 points over WS Audiology), and the retail and implant positions are distant from the leaders.

  • Hearing-aid wholesale (about 48% to 52% of group revenue; USD 7.7bn market): number one at 25.3% (Bernstein, 2025) or 29% (Jefferies, November 2026), ahead of WS Audiology (23.5% or 25%), Demant (21.5% or 19%), GN (17.0% or 13%) and Starkey (6.8%). The higher Jefferies figure reflects eleven consecutive quarters of gains; I would use about 27% for late 2026. In the US VA (about 15% of US units), Sonova holds 53.5% of value (September 2026) against Demant 23.8% and Starkey 9.6%; in Costco it has passed 20% of units since March 2026.
  • Retail (about 39% to 41% of revenue; USD 23bn market): about 7% global share, tied with Demant behind Amplifon (11% to 13%, over 10,000 points of sale); about 3,600 own clinics (AudioNova, GEERS, Boots Hearingcare and others). In Germany, its largest market, Sonova's GEERS (740 clinics, AudioNova 14% share) lost first place to Demant's KIND-enlarged network (over 900 clinics, about 12%; share measures differ).
  • Cochlear implants (about 7% of revenue; USD 2.0bn to 2.5bn market): Advanced Bionics at 15% to 16%, far behind Cochlear Ltd (60% to 65%) and roughly level with MED-EL.

For the model: wholesale is the profit engine (EBIT margin above 30% for leading makers) and the part where Sonova leads; retail and implants are scale positions where it is a follower.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

A851st of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: wholesale is one of the most concentrated markets in medtech: the top five hold over 94% (Bernstein) or over 85% (other sources), so the top five hold many times (more than ten times) the share of numbers six to ten, which are small regional or Chinese makers. Retail is fragmented; implants are a near-monopoly with two followers.

Wholesale: five makers, Sonova, WS Audiology, Demant, GN and Starkey; the first four each hold 13% to 29%. The industry consolidated from six majors to five; Amplifon's purchase of GN does not reduce the number of makers but changes one maker's ownership. Entry barriers are decade-long chip and software development, regulatory approval (EU MDR, FDA class II), audiologist training and fitting-software familiarity, and global service networks. Retail: independents still control 40% to 50% of global value; specialty chains about 37%; Amplifon 11% to 13%, Sonova and Demant about 7% each; optical chains (Fielmann 412 shop-in-shops; Specsavers over 1,700 joint clinics) and Costco (about 5% of global retail, over 15% of US distribution) are rising. Implants: Cochlear Ltd 60% to 65%, Advanced Bionics 15% to 16%, MED-EL most of the rest.

What it means for the model: wholesale concentration supports stable prices, high gross margins (70% to 80% at the makers) and rational launch-cycle competition. Retail fragmentation supports a long runway for bolt-ons.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

B+723rd of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the set of players is very stable and new entrants have taken essentially nothing; individual shares rotate by 2 to 5 points with each two-year product cycle, so stability is high at the group level and moderate at the company level.

New entrants: OTC devices (Bose, Sony, Lucid, Sennheiser, Jabra Enhance) have about 1% of US units four years after the rule; Sony and Lucid withdrew; Apple's AirPods Pro function and EssilorLuxottica's Nuance glasses are funnels, not share takers. No Chinese maker has broken into the developed-market premium tier.

Rotation: share moves follow platforms. Sonova gained with Marvel (2018), lost ground around 2020 to 2023 when Demant's Intent and others led, regained strongly with Infinio and Sphere (2024 to 2026: eleven straight quarters, "largest share gains since Marvel"); GN lost VA share from 21% to about 9% to 10% over several years; Starkey lost 600 basis points of VA share recently; Demant's Zeal took 13 points of VA custom value share in a quarter. Bernstein's rule: technology leads are caught up within one to two cycles and shares mean-revert; Sonova's standard deviation of organic growth (3.0%) is below Demant's (4.5%), which DNB Carnegie cites as the reason Sonova historically trades at a premium.

For the model: do not extrapolate the 2025 to 2026 share gains beyond FY2028; assume share gains of 1 to 2 points of growth in FY2027 to FY2028 falling to 0 to 1 point by FY2029 to FY2030, unless the next platform (about August 2028) also wins.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

B+705th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does market share consolidate towards the largest and most advantaged competitors, and is this company among them?

Judgement: share consolidates toward the makers with the best current platform and the deepest R&D (the "most advantaged"), and, structurally, toward vertically integrated makers with captive retail; Sonova is among the two most advantaged (with Demant) on both counts.

Evidence of the pull to the advantaged. The industry went from six to five makers; GN, the weakest R&D spender, has lost share for years and is being absorbed by a retailer; Starkey and GN rely on price promotions and service; AI silicon raises the R&D bar further (Sonova and Demant spend about 6% and about 9% of sales on R&D). Demant and Sonova have taken the VA from GN and Starkey.

Evidence of the pull to the integrated. Sonova (about 15% of its own wholesale volume through owned retail), Demant (over 4,500 clinics, KIND conversion to over 95% own product, internal wholesale +19% in Q1 2026) and now Amplifon-GN (internal sourcing from 40% to about 90%) are all integrating. Independents (52% of Demant's wholesale, 43% of Sonova's) are both the prize and the risk: they resent integrated competitors, which may favour makers seen as less threatening; Sonova, with retail at 39% of revenue, is not "untainted", but expert calls see it as the main beneficiary of GN's loss.

Is Sonova among them? Yes, on scale, R&D, channel breadth (independents, VA, Costco, own retail, managed care) and balance sheet. The risk is that consolidation toward integration closes retail doors to Sonova's wholesale (Amplifon; KIND), which is the cost side of the trend.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

A744th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What is competitors' attitude to competition: margin and return first, or volume first; benign or willing to suffer losses; do prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: margin-first and broadly benign. The industry competes through two-year platform launches rather than price, and prices did not fall when volumes fell in 2023 to 2025.

Evidence. Wholesale competitors avoid destructive pricing by launching platforms every two years to support prices (Jefferies, DNB Carnegie); clinical channel checks confirm wholesale prices stayed stable across manufacturers through the 2025 soft market; Demant explicitly prices Zeal only in the top tier "to prioritise value creation over volume"; Demant targets a 17% to 19% EBIT margin and is deleveraging; GN's owner is exiting; WS Audiology is private equity-owned and margin-focused; manufacturers passed through over 10% price increases in 2025 for tariffs. The exceptions: Starkey and GN (ReSound) use rebates and promotions with independents; competitors introduce lower-tier software options in price-sensitive segments; Demant's 2024 decision to withhold its newest premium products from managed care (to protect price) backfired into a boycott, showing the industry will sacrifice volume for price.

Do prices fall when volume falls? Not at list level; they fall through mix (more managed care, Costco, public tenders) and through rebates on older platforms. Global ASPs rose about 1% in Q2 calendar 2026.

For the model: no list-price war in the base case; competitive pressure shows as share loss or rebates in independents, so model price and mix at +0.5 to 1.5 points rather than negative.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

C+628th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years?

Judgement: the attitude is stable at the level of price but has become harder at the level of share contests; I see no sign of "Michael O'Leary" instability or of a "shark attack" on pricing, but two developments raise the probability of more aggressive behaviour in independents over the next two years.

What has changed. (1) Launch density: four premium platforms in one window (August 2026), against staggered launches historically, compresses each maker's lead and raises marketing spend. (2) Amplifon-GN: GN's incentives shift from selling to independents to supplying its owner; once internal volume is secured, GN could price aggressively to retain independents (or, conversely, de-prioritise them); independents may boycott. (3) Demant's balance sheet is repairing quickly (3.4x to 3.0x in six months) and its buyback resumed, which reduces any incentive to buy volume. (4) Demant's managed-care episode (2024) shows how fast a maker can lose a channel.

Would anyone attack if they sensed weakness? The maker most likely to discount is Starkey (private, service-led, losing share) or GN under new ownership to defend its external book. Neither has the R&D or scale to sustain a price war against Sonova and Demant. Sonova's own response to Zeal was to hold price and let custom share fall (61% to 43% to 45% in the VA) while total VA share rose, which is a rational, non-reactive posture.

For the model: treat the attitude as stable; add a 1-in-5 scenario of rebate pressure in European independents costing 50 to 100 basis points of wholesale gross margin in FY2028.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

B+725th of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: strongly supportive (a five-maker oligopoly with price discipline, high barriers and structural growth), and moderately fragile in two specific places: the independent channel, as vertical integration spreads, and the technology lead, which must be re-won every two years.

Support: concentration above 85% to 94%; rational, margin-first competitors; prices held through a soft market; barriers that have kept OTC and consumer electronics at about 1% share; demographic growth of about 4% a year; gross margins of 70% to 80% at the maker level and Sonova at 74.7% (core, H1 FY2027).

Fragility: (1) Downstream consolidation: Amplifon-GN, Demant-KIND, Costco (over 15% of US distribution), buying groups and managed care all raise buyer power and divert volume to captive suppliers; Sonova loses about CHF 50m to 60m from Amplifon and some German volume. (2) Technology rotation: Zeal showed a gap is punished within a quarter. (3) Payers: German sickness funds, French reform, US Medicaid and managed care pressure prices in reimbursed channels.

For the model: the structure supports Sonova's wholesale gross margin and pricing; the fragilities show up as share and channel-mix risks, not as industry-wide price deflation. I would rate the support at about 7 out of 10 for the next three years.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?A702nd of 38full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

A683rd of 38

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a weak threat and getting weaker in the evidence; the theoretical long-run substitutes (gene or drug therapies for hearing loss, fully implantable devices) are more than five years away and partly in Sonova's own portfolio.

  • Do nothing / delay: the largest "substitute" is non-adoption (penetration 38% in developed medical channels; first-time buyers wait five to seven years). This is shrinking slowly as stigma falls (earbuds, AirPods) and as awareness of hearing loss and cognitive decline rises.
  • OTC and consumer audio: about 1% of the US market; high returns; serve mild loss and Medicaid adults without coverage; data show failed OTC trials push patients to premium prescription devices. Weakening as a threat.
  • Smart glasses with hearing (Nuance, Meta): open-ear, mild loss only; acoustic limits and poor optical-store training so far.
  • Cochlear implants: a substitute at the severe end, but Sonova owns Advanced Bionics; expanded candidacy is a positive.
  • Therapeutics: Sonova's Advanced Bionics partners with Spiral Therapeutics (inner-ear drug delivery); no approved regenerative therapy exists.

For the model: no substitute effect on volume in the forecast horizon; keep long-run volume growth at about 4%.

Company - Competitive advantage

C+668th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Sonova's competitive advantage is real, broad and mostly durable: switching costs in fitting software and workflows, the VA position, Roger, owned retail, the lowest production cost and the deepest custom-silicon capability in the industry. The VA, where prices are fixed and clinicians choose freely, gives the strongest evidence that Sonova's products are not inferior and are preferred in total (53.5% value share, +470 basis points year on year), while showing one category (premium custom) lost to Demant. Pricing power is demonstrated and used moderately: premiums at the top, stable lists, tariff pass-through, no discounting. The moat is a treadmill at its technological edge: about half of Sonova's growth above market comes from winning each two-year cycle, and the August 2026 window is the most crowded on record. For the model: assume the non-rotating moat sustains market-level growth and margins; give share gains and premium pricing a finite life (through FY2028) unless the next cycle is also won.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C+7010th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Sonova holds a layered moat: a strong, durable base of switching costs and channel positions that does not rotate, and a strong but temporary technology lead that must be re-won every two years. In late 2026 the technology layer widened against GN and Starkey and narrowed against Demant in custom devices; the channel layer narrowed modestly (Amplifon, Germany).

Customer-related (strong, stable). (1) Fitting-software and workflow familiarity: audiologists' switching cost is retraining their own workflow, which keeps shares sticky (Bernstein). (2) VA position: 53.5% of value on five-year fixed-price contracts, defended by clinicians' familiarity with Phonak software. (3) Roger accessory ecosystem: the default in schools and paediatrics. (4) Physical lock-in: custom moulds and periodic adjustments tie a patient to a device and a clinic for the five-to-six-year replacement cycle. (5) Owned retail (about 3,600 clinics, about 15% of Sonova's wholesale volume, 39% of revenue).

Production and scale (strong, widening). In-house silicon (DEEPSONIC, Hypersonic; 37% better power efficiency; experts cite a two-to-three-year lead in custom silicon), R&D of about CHF 218m a year, and manufacturing efficiency (production cost about 13.5% of sales against about 22% at Demant, Deutsche Bank). EON is the only new-hardware platform of the August 2026 wave.

Regulatory (moderate, stable). EU MDR and FDA approvals; professional-fitting rules that confined OTC to mild loss.

Knowledge and culture (moderate). The Berlin deep-neural-network team acquired in 2019 underpins the AI lead (key-person and poaching risk noted).

Widening or narrowing. Widening: silicon versus GN and Starkey; Costco presence; Asia. Narrowing: custom ITE versus Demant's Zeal (VA custom share 61% to 43% to 45%), Germany retail versus Demant-KIND, Amplifon as a channel, independents' service complaints. Net: broadly stable, with the "rotating" layer the swing factor.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

A743rd of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Sonova is the largest hearing-care maker (CHF 3.6bn continuing revenue, about 18,000 employees), roughly 1.2x to 1.3x Demant and 1.5x Amplifon in revenue, and about 3x to 4x GN Hearing; scale confers R&D absorption, manufacturing cost, the capacity to serve volume channels (VA, Costco) and the balance sheet to consolidate retail.

Scale comparison (approximate, converted by me where needed). Sonova CHF 3,606m (FY2026); Demant DKK 22,971m (about CHF 2.8bn to 2.9bn, but over 4,500 clinics and 47% retail); Amplifon EUR 2,396m (about CHF 2.2bn; over 10,000 points of sale; becoming about EUR 3.6bn pro forma with GN); GN Hearing about CHF 1bn; WS Audiology privately held, similar wholesale share to Sonova (Bernard ran it). Sonova's employee base: about 12,200 in sales, marketing and administration, about 5,000 in operations, about 1,270 in R&D.

What scale confers.

  • R&D: spending about CHF 218m (about 6% of sales) funds parallel chip, software, custom and cochlear programmes; AI raises the minimum efficient scale, which is why GN sold and Starkey loses share.
  • Manufacturing and procurement: lowest production cost in the industry; regional footprint (Switzerland, Vietnam, China, Mexico) for tariff and FX mitigation.
  • Channel breadth: supplying the VA (about 15% of US units), Costco (above 20% of its units) and over 100 countries at once.
  • Retail consolidation: free cash flow of about CHF 600m to 700m a year and 1.1x leverage, against constrained rivals.
  • Data: integrated retail and over 600,000 qualified leads a year.

Limit. Scale in retail is not a moat by itself (mid-teens margins, local density matters more than global size), and scale did not stop Demant's Zeal winning the custom category.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

C+669th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Sonova is demonstrably not inferior and, on the best natural experiment available, leading in total; it is inferior in one category (premium rechargeable custom) where Demant's Zeal now leads.

The natural experiment: the VA. In the US VA channel prices are fixed by central contract and veterans pay nothing, so share reflects clinicians' and patients' preference, not price. Sonova's total value share rose to 53.5% to 54% in July to September 2026 (+450 to 470 basis points year on year, a five-year high), after Zeal's entry; management said Infinio Sphere VA sales exceeded the total sales of its largest competitors. That disproves inferiority for the core RIC range. In the custom rechargeable category, Zeal took 28% and Sonova fell to 43% to 45% (Virto R had over 60% before Zeal), which shows Zeal's superior form factor and positioning there.

Clinician and patient evidence. Positive: speech-in-noise performance of Sphere and EON (clinicians report reduced listening effort), Roger integration, the EasyGuard wax membrane (up to 38% fewer service visits), largest prescription share (50% to 60%) in expert practices surveyed, largest paediatric share. Negative: original Sphere bulk and battery drain (addressed by the AutoSense 7.0 update and by EON, 25% smaller and 20% lighter), receiver breakages at launch, a 6.6% fall in the reliability rate, repair delays and a "nickel-and-dime" support culture cited by some private practices; Starkey praised for service.

Alternatives. OTC and consumer devices are inferior for moderate-to-severe loss (high returns).

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

B+646th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility?

Judgement: demonstrable and used, but deliberately moderate: Sonova earns real premiums at the top of its range (10% for Sphere), held list prices through a soft market, passed through tariff costs, and refuses to discount to independents; it has chosen share over price in the VA and accepts lower prices in volume channels. Erosion of utility is minimal in total share data.

Applied evidence. (1) Sphere at a 10% premium to Infinio in the VA while total VA share rose. (2) Wholesale list prices stable across the 2023 to 2025 downturn. (3) Industry passed through over 10% price rises in 2025, Phonak "aggressively". (4) An expert says Sonova used its number-one status to raise prices through cycles. (5) Channel checks: Phonak is among the least aggressive on price cuts and promotions. (6) Gross margin of 73.7% to 74.7% maintained through a dual-chip platform costing about 10% more to build. (7) Retail ASPs positive in H1 FY2027.

Limits. Demant's Zeal shows more pricing power in custom (42% premium, USD 695 against Sonova's USD 540 VA average): Sonova's premium is narrower. Volume channels (Costco, managed care, VA) carry contract prices; payers cap reimbursed prices.

Pricing power across conditions: strong in private-pay premium (launch premiums), moderate in independents (stable lists, rebates elsewhere), weak in public tenders and managed care (contract-set), and untested in a tariff shock (2025 suggests partial pass-through).

For the model: wholesale like-for-like price +1 to 2 points a year in premium tiers, group ASP +0.5 to 1 point after channel mix; no premium beyond 10% on EON in the base case.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

C+4810th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers?

Judgement: little, in stated strategy or culture; Sonova is a margin-keeper. The only de facto sharing is through low-priced volume channels and planned "made for Asia" products, both driven by channel economics rather than a philosophy of passing savings to customers.

Evidence against sharing: Sonova refuses to negotiate prices with independents; its stated aims are premium pricing and margin expansion (core EBIT CAGR above sales CAGR; CHF 90m savings to the margin bridge, partly reinvested in R&D and IT); some independents describe a transactional support culture. Evidence of de facto sharing: the Sennheiser-branded Costco range at USD 300 to 400 (high-20s EBIT margin, so Sonova still earns well); simpler, cheaper devices for Asian markets; EasyGuard reducing clinics' service time (a cost saving shared with audiologists); VA contracts at USD 480 to 540.

Implication. In the Wisdom Library's "scale economies shared" model, sharing builds durable share at the cost of near-term margin. Sonova has the opposite posture, which maximises near-term margin and is consistent with the industry's margin-first conduct, but leaves it exposed to a lower-priced rival in independents and to buyers who resent the posture. Not a weakness for an 18-month model; a modest negative for the durability of share in independents.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C-5818th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and financial outputs each period?

Judgement: mechanical forces carry the base (demographics, oligopoly pricing, installed-base replacement, VA contract inertia, software familiarity), giving about 4% volume plus 1 point of price without heroics; but share gains and margin expansion above that require a heroic re-win of the technology lead every two years (a treadmill), plus self-help on cost and the franc.

Mechanical (each period, without effort): ageing populations and rising adoption; replacement cycles of five to six years on a large installed base (Germany 47%, France 55% adoption); oligopoly price discipline; the VA's five-year contracts and audiologists' software familiarity; integrated retail capturing about 15% of own wholesale volume; operating leverage on a largely fixed R&D and overhead base; the CHF 90m savings programme once launched.

Heroic (must be re-won): platform leadership (Marvel, Paradise, Lumity, Infinio, EON each needed to beat rivals; Zeal shows a gap is punished within a quarter); custom-category leadership (instant-fit launch); retail lead-generation efficiency as customer acquisition costs rise; Asia build; implant recovery (Integro approval).

Mechanical negative: the franc's long-run appreciation, which has subtracted about 2 to 3 points a year from reported growth, works against Sonova every period unless the cost base moves.

For the model: mechanical forces alone give about 4% to 5% local-currency revenue growth and roughly flat margins; the heroic layer is worth 2 to 3 points of growth and 50 to 100 basis points of margin a year when it works.

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

Judgement: a good, sensible strategy with the right priorities (innovation for adoption, local commercial execution, operational excellence), bolder than the previous one in organisation and Asia, but conventional in its two biggest bets (retail roll-up and premium platforms), and it does not yet address the custom-category gap with a launched product.

Right. (1) Exiting Consumer Hearing removed a failed adjacency. (2) The four-region model puts wholesale and retail under one regional owner, which suits an industry where local channel relationships decide share. Not great yet. The custom category was ceded to Zeal without a timely response (instant-fit "within 18 months"); retail M&A has no disclosed return discipline; CHF 6bn depends on undefined acquisitions.

C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

A624th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company put shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent is its governance, and why?

Judgement: governance is prudent and shareholder-friendly in structure (single share class, fully independent board, clean audits, tightened shareholding requirements, clawback, insider buying); shareholder returns are currently subordinated to management's growth agenda (buyback suspended to fund retail M&A without a disclosed hurdle), and long-term pay is moving from a return-on-capital link to a relative-TSR link. On balance, shareholders come first in governance and second in capital allocation for now.

Evidence for. 59,626,809 registered shares of one class; all eight directors independent (Demant 60%); the internal audit head reports to the audit chair; Ernst & Young unqualified opinions, no significant control deficiencies; related-party dealings immaterial; no anti-corruption violations; shareholding requirements of 300% (chair) and 200% (others) of base fee from FY2027, with a five-year build-up and three-year blocking on restricted shares; clawback and malus for three years; board holdings up from 34,512 to 53,378 shares with zero net sales; the chair +130%; the chief executive's base salary of CHF 1.0m with a 109.44% FY2026 payout.

Evidence against. The 10% voting cap could entrench management; the CHF 1.5bn buyback is paused at 1.1x leverage to fund acquisitions; no ROIC hurdle or post-deal review is published; performance options linked to ROCE are discontinued in favour of PSUs on relative TSR; average board tenure was skewed by one 17-year director (now gone).

Why. The board's priority is to finance a share-gain and retail strategy while rivals are constrained; that is defensible but should come with disclosed return discipline.

Dividend: progressive (CHF 3.20 to 4.70 over five years), 40% to 45% payout.

Now. Leverage will fall below 1.0x by FY2028 absent action; the chief executive emphasises return on capital verbally, but the next allocation choice (larger retail M&A against buybacks) is where his record will be set.

Fair multiple. A business with 19% to 21% ROCE, 6% to 7% organic growth and limited reinvestment runway justifies roughly its ten-year median of 20.7x two-year-forward earnings (bands: 16.8x at the 10th percentile, 23.1x at the 75th); the current 21.2x is at fair value on that basis, not cheap.

Judgement: moderately clean. The gap. FY2026: normalised EBITA CHF 811.2m against reported EBITA CHF 724m (gap CHF 87m: a CHF 34.7m software impairment, CHF 16.7m restructuring, about CHF 24m of cochlear product-liability provisions and other items); normalised EPS CHF 10.42 against reported continuing EPS CHF 9.02 and reported basic EPS CHF 7.23 (after the Consumer Hearing loss of CHF 106.5m). About CHF 0.50 a share after tax recurs.

Capitalisation. Development costs are expensed except tooling, equipment and cochlear implants (conservative). IT capitalisation produced the CHF 35m impairment.

Tax. FactSet reported tax rate 2.6% to 8.9% (FY2020 to FY2024), 17.6% (FY2025), 16.0% (FY2026). The step-up is consistent with Swiss tax reform and the OECD global minimum tax; a drift to 18% to 20% is plausible (the memo's sensitivity used 20%) and would cost 2% to 5% of EPS.

Other leakage. Share count flat (no buybacks; option exercises small, CHF 6.2m in FY2026); minorities small (CHF 20.5m); FX transaction losses in net financial expense when hedging costs rise.

For the model: forecast core EPS but carry restructuring of CHF 30m to 40m a year in reported EPS through FY2029 and a tax rate of 17% rising to 18%.

5a

Pricing power

3 answers

C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?B+646th of 38full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

B+584th of 26

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price and mix contribute about 1 point a year of Sonova's 5.5% to 6.5% local-currency growth (about 1.5 points in FY2027 on EON and VA mix, 0.75 to 1.0 point thereafter, rising again with the next platform around August 2028). In the margin it is a defence, not a driver: about one point of price (CHF 39m to 45m a year) roughly offsets 2.5% to 3% inflation on an operating-cost base of about CHF 2.0bn. Margin expansion must come from volume leverage and the savings programme. The pricing risk in the window is to share in independents, not to list price.

Evidence of real, held price. Phonak Infinio Sphere carries a 10% premium over standard Infinio and is defended in the US Veterans Affairs channel; EON is positioned at the premium tier; manufacturers held list increases of 10% to 17% through the 2022 to 2023 inflation; suppliers raised wholesale prices by about 5% in 2025 (channel checks, July 2026); the 2025 Veterans Affairs contract reset brought a weighted-average price increase of 26% for Sonova (14% Demant, 10% GN), fixed for five years; Sonova's retail growth has been "more skewed toward price than volume" (November 2025) and retail ASP was positive in H1 FY2027.

What dilutes it. (1) Channel mix: independents pay USD 600 to 1,100 per unit, Costco USD 300 to 400, large chains USD 150 to 200; wholesale ASP trends were flat to negative in 2023 to 2025 entirely through mix (Redburn Atlantic). (2) Demant's Zeal earns USD 692 in the Veterans Affairs channel against Sonova's USD 540, because Demant priced Zeal 42% above its standard device; Sonova chose volume share at a 10% premium. (3) Reimbursement caps: German statutory funds about EUR 800 per aid; France's Class I cap EUR 950 per ear; Medicare Advantage and Medicaid pressure in the US. (4) Rival rebates: Oticon, ReSound, Starkey and Signia offer volume rebates and promotions; Phonak refuses to bend on list price and instead discounts older models.

The point the Consumer should carry: ASP is a misleading indicator for Sonova. Costco lowers group ASP but carries a high-20s EBIT margin because the cost to serve is low; managed care lowers ASP and margin together. Group revenue per unit can therefore fall while profit rises. Monitor wholesale gross margin and VA value share, not ASP.

By condition, for the model.

  • Crowded launch window (now to mid-2027): list prices hold; rivals compete through rebates and lower software tiers. Phonak's rigidity risks prescription share in US and European independents (channel checks in Michigan, July 2026, describe clinics exploring other brands). I carry no price cut; I carry share gains fading (P-05). A rebate war in European independents is my 1-in-5 downside: 50 to 100 basis points of wholesale gross margin, CHF 10m to 20m of EBIT.
  • Soft private-pay demand: hits volume and trading-down (about 9% of US private-pay patients in 2023), not list price.
  • Veterans Affairs: prices are fixed by the 2025 contracts until about 2030, so this channel's price contribution is zero until then; share is the variable.
  • US tariffs (Section 232): hearing aids were exempt in 2025 under the Nairobi Protocol; it is unclear whether Section 232 overrides it. If a 15% to 20% duty applied to Sonova's US imports (about CHF 150m to 200m of landed cost, my estimate), the gross cost would be CHF 25m to 40m a year (3% to 4.5% of EBIT); with a price response of 5% to 10% after a lag of several months and the Mexicali shift, the first-year net hit would be CHF 15m to 25m, mostly recovered in the second year. This is a scenario, not a base-case input.
  • Strong franc: prices are set in local currencies, so the franc cannot be priced away; price is not a currency hedge.

Margin arithmetic. FY2028: price and mix about +1 point (about CHF 40m) against inflation on personnel and other operating costs of about CHF 50m; savings and volume leverage provide the margin gain. If price fell to zero for a year, core EBIT growth would drop by about 4 to 5 points and margin expansion would stop. If EON proved clearly superior and Sonova followed Demant with a top-tier custom device (instant-fit, "within 18 months"), latent price of 1 to 2 points would appear in FY2029 to FY2030; I do not model it.

Peers. Straumann (patient-paid premium dental) shows the same structure: premium price holds, trading down grows in value tiers. Cochlear Ltd shows what a payer-driven reset does: China's volume-based procurement cut implant list prices about 75%; Advanced Bionics is exposed to the same in China (part of why Cochlear Implants fell 11.1% in FY2026).

Operating leverage

C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

A643rd of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: material: with a gross margin near 75% and largely fixed R&D, overhead and clinic costs, each 1 point of local-currency revenue growth moves core EBIT by roughly 1.5% to 2.5% (volume) and by up to 3% to 3.5% (price); a plausible range of ±2 points of growth around the base therefore moves EBIT by about ±4% to 6% a year.

Evidence. H1 FY2027: revenue +CHF 115m in francs, core EBIT +CHF 68m, an incremental margin of about 59% (flattered by the end of ramp costs; underlying I estimate 40% to 45%). The Change Forces report's calculated sensitivities: +1 point of constant-currency revenue growth lifts core operating profit about 1.5% and core EPS about 1.3%; +2 points of volume at a 74.7% gross margin lifts core EPS about 3.8%; +0.5 points of margin is about CHF 20m of core operating profit and 2.3% of core EPS. Historical precedent of negative leverage: FY2024 (revenue -3.0% in francs, normalised EBITA -8.2%, after losing a large US customer) and H1 FY2026 (Mexicali costs, EBITA 18.4% margin).

Cost structure (my estimate on FY2027 revenue of about CHF 3.9bn). Cost of sales about 25% (largely variable materials, partly fixed plant); R&D about 6% (fixed); retail operating costs (audiologists, rent, marketing) about 25% to 28% of group sales (semi-fixed in the short term); wholesale selling and group administration about 15% (mostly fixed). Fixed and semi-fixed costs are therefore about 45% to 50% of sales.

Range of outcomes for FY2028 (illustration). Base local-currency growth 6.5%: core EBIT about +9% to 10%. Growth of 4.5% (launch window lost, market fade): EBIT about +4% to 6%. Growth of 8.5% (window won): EBIT about +13% to 15%. Add franc translation at about 1.6x the sales effect.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

A632nd of 26

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: operating leverage is material in local currency (each point of local-currency growth moves core EBIT by about 1.6% to 1.9%), but over FY2021 to FY2026 it was invisible in francs because translation absorbed it and management recycled part of it into reinvestment. Over FY2027 to FY2031 I expect it to show: core EBIT margin from about 21.6% to 24.2% (+260 basis points), of which +120 basis points in FY2027 alone and about +35 basis points a year after, with core EBIT growing about 1.3 to 1.4 times revenue in francs against roughly 1.0 times (flat margins) over FY2022 to FY2026. It is "managed" leverage: management's own margin bridge deliberately gives back 1 to 3 points of it, so it will not run at its mechanical rate.

How much leverage the cost base carries. On FY2027 revenue of about CHF 3.9bn: cost of sales about 25% (mostly variable materials, partly fixed plant); R&D about 6% (fixed); retail clinic costs about 25% to 28% of group sales (audiologist pay about 30% of clinic sales, rent, marketing; semi-fixed in the short run, with customer acquisition costs rising); wholesale selling and group administration about 15% (largely fixed). Fixed and semi-fixed costs are therefore about 45% to 50% of sales. The underlying incremental margin is about 35% to 40% on volume and close to 100% on price. Applied: ±1 point of local-currency growth is about ±CHF 39m of revenue and about ±CHF 14m to 17m of core EBIT (±1.6% to 1.9%), about ±1.7% of EPS. The segments differ sharply: wholesale (core gross margin above 75%, EBIT margin above 30%) has an incremental margin above 50%; retail (mid-teens margin) about 20% to 25%; Cochlear Implants currently has negative leverage running in reverse (H1 FY2027 sales -10.2% in local currency, core EBIT halved from CHF 13.9m to CHF 7.1m, a decremental margin of about 50%), which means its recovery is the most leveraged line in the group.

What the last six years show. In local currency the leverage worked: FY2026 normalised EBITA +17.3% on sales +5.9% (about 2.9 times). In francs it did not: the same year EBITA rose 3.7% because translation took CHF 221m of sales and CHF 106.5m of EBITA (150 basis points of margin). Negative leverage is also real: FY2024 sales -3.0% in francs, normalised EBITA -8.2% (2.7 times, the Costco contract loss). The one counter-example is instructive: in FY2021 (COVID, sales -6.8% in local currency) the margin rose from 21.2% to 23.2% because management cut discretionary costs and took subsidies quickly, which shows a downside lever of roughly CHF 50m to 100m of flexible cost. The FactSet adjusted EBIT margin moved only within 19.1% to 23.3% over FY2021 to FY2026, so in francs the business has been a flat-margin business for six years.

H1 FY2027 decomposed (this answers the AlphaSense report's central question). Revenue +CHF 115m in francs, core EBIT +CHF 68m (a 59% incremental margin). The non-repeat of Mexicali ramp-up and logistics costs was worth CHF 10m to 15m (Morgan Stanley), i.e. 55 to 80 basis points of the 250-basis-point margin gain. The remaining 170 to 195 basis points came from volume leverage, positive price in wholesale and retail, and premium mix. So about two-thirds of the gain is lasting. H2 FY2027 has no ramp-cost base effect (H2 FY2026 was already clean at a core EBIT margin of about 24.6%) and carries a low double-digit CHF million strategic investment step-up (R&D, IT, digital, operations) that management describes as recurring.

How I translate it into the line items.

  • Core gross margin: 73.7% (FY2026, depressed by ramp costs in H1) to about 75.1% (FY2027), then about 75.3%, 75.5%, 75.8%, 76.0% (FY2028 to FY2031): footprint savings, premium mix and the end of ramp costs, offset by retail growing faster than wholesale, Costco and the costlier new chips.
  • Core EBIT margin: 22.8% (FY2027; H1 20.9%, H2 about 24.5%, 10 basis points below H2 FY2026), 23.2%, 23.5%, 23.9%, 24.2%. FY2028 to FY2031 adds about 35 basis points a year: volume leverage +40 to 45, net savings +10 to 25, Cochlear Implants recovery +10, annualisation of the H2 FY2027 investment step-up -30 (FY2028 only), retail mix -10, franc transaction effect about -13.
  • EBITDA margin: 28.3%, 28.7%, 29.0%, 29.4%, 29.7% (depreciation and amortisation steady at about 5.5% of sales as capex rises to the 3.5% to 4.0% target).
  • Ratio of core EBIT growth to revenue growth in francs: 1.8 times in FY2027 (helped by ramp-cost relief), then about 1.3 to 1.4 times.

Why I do not model more. (1) The H2 FY2027 step-up (about CHF 12m to 15m in the half, CHF 25m to 30m annualised) is a deliberate recycling of leverage; Morgan Stanley puts it at 60 basis points of H2 margin, I put the net H2 effect at 10 to 20 basis points because volume leverage continues. (2) Management's mid-term margin bridge reserves 1 to 3 points for reinvestment against +2 to 3 from footprint and +1 to 2 from leverage. (3) Retail bolt-ons at mid-teens margins and rising customer acquisition costs (an industry benchmark of USD 450 per new client and expert estimates of USD 700 to 800) dilute. (4) Wage inflation in clinics is structural. The company's own algorithm (sales 5% to 10%, core EBIT 7% to 12%) implies about 40 basis points a year at the midpoints; my 35 basis points after franc drag is consistent with it.

Cone. For FY2028, ±2 points of local-currency growth around my 6.3% moves core EBIT by about ±4% to 5%; adding a franc swing of ±3 points (at 1.6 times) widens it to about ±9%. Peers: Demant, with retail at about 50% of revenue, carries more rigid retail costs and less wholesale leverage; Amplifon's fixed costs are about 50% of sales.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7672P-0260
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?B728th of 38full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C+606th of 26

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: debt is irrelevant as a risk and nearly irrelevant as an EPS amplifier; the balance sheet matters through its under-use. On my cash flows, net debt to EBITDA falls to about 0.7x by March 2027, below the 1.0x to 1.5x corridor a year earlier than the first set expected, and to about 0.3x to 0.5x by FY2029 to FY2031 even with CHF 300m a year of buybacks. The single largest EPS lever outside operations is therefore how management deploys about CHF 0.35bn of headroom at March 2027, rising to over CHF 1bn by FY2030. I model a modest buyback as the base; a larger retail acquisition, which management prefers, would be more EPS-accretive and margin-dilutive.

The numbers. Net debt (including leases, which FactSet and, from the matching figures, the company include) was CHF 1,072.4m at 30-Sep-26 after the CHF 279.8m dividend paid in June, 1.1x. H2 cash generation is seasonally strong (net debt fell by about CHF 325m in H2 FY2026). My H2 FY2027 free cash flow after lease payments of about CHF 370m, less about CHF 70m of bolt-ons, takes net debt to about CHF 735m at March 2027, 0.67x on FY2027 EBITDA of about CHF 1,095m. Without any buyback or larger deal, net debt would be close to zero by FY2030. Net financial costs are small: about CHF 23m to 30m a year historically (FactSet net interest CHF 29.7m in FY2026, including lease interest of CHF 6.8m), only CHF 1.7m in H1 FY2027 because of foreign-exchange and valuation gains. I model CHF -14m for FY2027 and CHF -22m to -25m thereafter, about 2.3% of core EBIT: a 10% move in EBIT moves EPS by about 10.3%. Interest cover is about 40 times; peers are far more levered (Demant 3.0x, Amplifon about 3x after GN, GN 3.8x before its sale).

Base case for the model (a judgement, explicitly a proxy). No buyback in FY2027 (management has said so). Buybacks of CHF 150m in FY2028 (an announcement at the May 2027 results is likely once leverage is visibly below 1.0x, partial-year execution), then CHF 300m a year in FY2029 to FY2031. Effect: average shares fall from 59.5m to about 57.1m by FY2031; EPS is about 2% higher in FY2030 and 3% higher in FY2031 than without buybacks, net of interest forgone. Even so, leverage keeps falling (0.51x, 0.46x, 0.39x, 0.31x), so my base case still under-deploys the balance sheet. I leave the residual undeployed rather than model an unannounced acquisition.

Which deployment is more accretive (the insight for the third and fourth sets). A buyback at about CHF 300 (21 times forward earnings) buys an earnings yield of about 4.7%. A retail bolt-on at roughly 1.5 times sales and a mid-teens EBIT margin costs about 10 to 11 times EBIT: a pre-tax yield of about 9% to 10%, about 7.5% to 8% after tax, against after-tax debt cost of about 1.5%. Per franc, acquisitions are therefore about 1.5 times as EPS-accretive as buybacks at today's multiple. A CHF 0.7bn to 1.0bn retail deal would add roughly CHF 450m to 650m of revenue, about +5% to +7% to EPS, but take 60 to 80 basis points off the group margin and pull ROCE (20.9% in H1 FY2027) toward 18%. Management's preference for "larger M&A" (Bernstein, November 2026: no buyback in H2 because of the M&A pipeline) is rational for EPS and the CHF 6bn ambition; its cost appears in margin, ROCE and probably the multiple. A buyback is the cleaner signal for the market. Either way, the balance sheet is a source of upside to EPS from FY2028, not of risk.

The balance sheet path. Equity rises from about CHF 2.6bn toward CHF 3.6bn to 3.8bn by FY2031 (before franc translation of foreign equity) (after dividends at a 43% payout and the base-case buybacks); working capital stays at about 20% to 20.5% of revenue; capex rises to about 3.7% of sales. If nothing is deployed, ROCE drifts up (cash is excluded from capital employed) but return on equity falls, and the "lazy balance sheet" becomes a narrative item.

Peers and precedent. Demant resumed buybacks on 9-Oct-26 (at least DKK 3bn by end-2027) at 3.0x leverage; GN plans to distribute at least DKK 4bn after the GN Hearing sale and has adopted the same 1.0x to 1.5x corridor as Sonova. Sonova's own history is pro-cyclical: buybacks of CHF 732m (FY2022) and CHF 486m (FY2023) at 25 to 28 times earnings, none at 16 to 18 times. Peers with more debt are returning more cash than Sonova, which will sharpen investor pressure at the May 2027 results.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7756P-0360
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C5614th of 38

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: about 3% to 5% of sales is credibly reducible over four years without harming growth (the CHF 90m programme plus franc cost relocation and some retail efficiency); the remainder is either variable, strategic (R&D) or tied to clinic service, and management intends to reinvest part of the savings.

By cost block (my estimate, FY2027 revenue about CHF 3.9bn).

  • Cost of sales (about 25% of sales, CHF 0.95bn to 1.0bn): reducible by footprint optimisation (Mexicali, Vietnam), automation, value engineering, multi-sourcing; the programme's +2 to 3 points of margin from footprint is mostly here. Cuttable: about 10% to 15% of this block over four years.
  • R&D (about 6%, CHF 220m to 240m): strategic; the regional R&D in Asia and the AI chip roadmap need more, not less; R&D grew 4.2% in H1 FY2027 while falling to 5.9% of sales. Cuttable: none; it will rise in francs.
  • Retail operating costs (about 25% to 28% of sales): audiologist labour (about 30% of a clinic's sales in the industry), rent and marketing; lead-generation costs are rising; efficiency comes from density, internal lead generation (600,000 leads), AI scheduling and remote fitting. Cuttable: 1 to 2 points of retail margin over time, offset by wage inflation.
  • Group overhead and wholesale selling (about 15%): the Swiss headquarters (headcount down 8% in FY2026), legacy business-unit duplication removed by the regional model, IT (after the CHF 35m impairment). Cuttable: about 5% to 10% of this block.
  • Swiss franc costs (about 15% of costs): moving to below 10% is both saving and hedge.

Net to margin. Gross savings of CHF 90m (about 2.3% of sales) by FY2030, net from FY2028; the margin bridge reserves 1 to 3 points for reinvestment, so the net margin gain from cost is about 1 to 2 points by FY2030.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

A601st of 26

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the CHF 90m transformation programme is credible on delivery, but only about half of it will reach core EBIT, because management has built the reinvestment into the plan. I expect gross savings of about CHF 10m (FY2027), 30m (FY2028), 60m (FY2029), 85m to 90m (FY2030), and net savings at core EBIT of about CHF 0m, 10m, 25m, 40m to 45m, i.e. roughly +100 to 110 basis points of margin by FY2030, back-end loaded into FY2029 and FY2030 and mostly visible in the gross margin. Separately, the non-repeat of the Mexicali ramp-up costs (CHF 10m to 15m) is a one-time FY2027 gain already in the numbers. The cash cost of the programme sits outside core earnings and is real: about CHF 0.45 to 0.55 a share of reported EPS a year in FY2027 and FY2028.

What is known about the programme. CHF 50m of one-off cost over three years (restructuring, excluded from core EBIT) for CHF 90m of annual run-rate savings by year four, net savings from year two (FY2028); levers are footprint optimisation (Mexicali, Vietnam), manufacturing automation, value engineering and procurement (Morgan Stanley: procurement adds 2 to 3 points to FY2027 core EBIT growth). The franc share of costs is to fall from about 15% to below 10%. Management's margin bridge: +2 to 3 points from footprint, +1 to 2 from operating leverage, minus 1 to 3 points of strategic investment.

Why I believe delivery. Sonova delivered CHF 40m of run-rate savings in FY2025 from its previous programme; the new operations chief ran WS Audiology's global operations; peer programmes of the same type have been delivered on time or early: Demant raised its 2026 savings to DKK 300m from DKK 250m (the DKK 500m programme, full effect 2028), GN's One-GN realised DKK 430m in 2024 ahead of plan, and Amplifon's Fit4Growth cost EUR 25m instead of EUR 35m while targeting 150 to 200 basis points of margin. The base rate for manufacturing and procurement programmes is good; the base rate for retail cost-cutting is poor (closing clinics loses referrals).

Why only half reaches EBIT. (1) Management has already started reinvesting: the H2 FY2027 step-up of a low double-digit CHF million, recurring, is about CHF 25m to 30m annualised, roughly the savings of FY2028. (2) The mid-term bridge reserves 1 to 3 points for reinvestment. (3) Cost inflation in the uncuttable lines continues: audiologist pay (about 30% of clinic sales), customer acquisition (rising), R&D (rising with AI silicon). (4) Footprint moves carry transition costs inside core gross margin, as Mexicali showed (80 to 100 basis points in H1 FY2026); the next phase (automation, further transfers) can repeat that on a smaller scale. Demant shows the same pattern: savings on time, special items raised from DKK 200m to DKK 250m.

Which lines. Cuttable: cost of sales (footprint, automation, procurement; about 10% to 15% of that block over four years), Swiss headquarters and the duplicated business-unit structure removed by the regional model, IT after the CHF 35m impairment (about 5% to 10% of overhead). Not cuttable: R&D (about 6% of sales; it will rise in francs), audiologist labour, lead generation. So the savings appear mostly in gross margin (my 75.1% to 76.0% path) and in a stable rather than rising overhead ratio.

The franc cost relocation is a hedge more than a saving. Moving the franc cost share from 15% to below 10% shifts roughly CHF 150m to 200m of costs into the currencies of sales. It does not change the translation effect, but it cuts the transaction mismatch by about one-third: after it, a 5% franc appreciation costs roughly CHF 7m to 10m less EBIT. It is a FY2029 to FY2031 benefit and will not show as a separate line.

Reported against core. Restructuring and transaction costs: CHF 44m (FY2025), CHF 36.4m in H1 FY2026, CHF 12.0m in H1 FY2027 (restructuring 6.7m, cochlear product liability 5.0m), CHF 35m to 40m guided for FY2027. I carry about CHF 38m (FY2027), 30m (FY2028) and 20m to 25m a year after (retail integration and recurring "one-offs"), i.e. reported continuing EPS about CHF 0.53 below core in FY2027 and CHF 0.30 to 0.35 below thereafter. The Consumer should treat the core-to-reported gap as a permanent feature (it has existed every year since at least FY2020), not as a fading one.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4758P-0555
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?C-5818th of 38full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C+557th of 26

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: reinforcing loops dominate FY2027 and FY2028 (the platform-share loop around EON and the Veterans Affairs channel, the rising minimum efficient scale of AI silicon, and a cash-generation loop that builds deployable capacity); balancing loops dominate FY2029 to FY2031 (mean reversion of share within one to two platform cycles, rising retail customer acquisition costs, channel conflict with independents, and management's deliberate reinvestment of savings). The model should therefore show wholesale growth fading from about 9.5% (FY2027) to 4.5% to 5.5%, margin increments shrinking after FY2027, and cash accumulating faster than it can be deployed at high returns.

Reinforcing loops.

  • R1, the platform-share loop: a winning platform gains Veterans Affairs and independent prescriptions, pulls through retail, funds R&D and the next platform. Precedent: Audéo Marvel (2018 to 2019) lifted Sonova's VA share from 39% to 53% by 2020, held through Paradise (2020) and Lumity (2022); the Infinio cycle (2024) gave eleven consecutive quarters of wholesale share gains and a VA value share of 53.5% (September 2026). What stops it: rival catch-up (Bernstein: leads are matched within one to two cycles), execution slips (Infinio receiver durability, original Sphere bulk), and a category gap (Zeal cut Sonova's VA custom share from 61% to 43% to 48%). Status now: EON is the only new-chip platform of the August 2026 wave; conversion is faster than management's baseline. Financial effect: wholesale share gains of about 3 to 4 points above market in FY2027 (Costco, VA, Virto R, EON), about 1.5 points in FY2028, 0.5 to 1.0 thereafter (a step up in FY2030 if the next platform, due on a two-year cadence around August 2028, also wins).
  • R2, scale in AI silicon: R&D of about USD 250m (Sonova) against USD 190m to 200m (Demant, WS Audiology) and USD 120m (GN) raises the minimum efficient scale; GN has sold, Starkey loses share. Slow-acting; it supports Sonova's share durability and lets R&D fall slightly as a share of sales (5.9% in H1 FY2027) while rising in francs.
  • R3, cash builds capacity: strong operating cash flow plus paused buybacks drive leverage below the corridor (P-02), which forces a deployment decision that lifts EPS. This loop is the strongest one in the next 18 months.
  • R4, owned retail secures volume and data: about 15% of Sonova's wholesale volume goes through its own clinics; bolt-ons extend that. Weak, and offset by B2.

Balancing loops.

  • B1, crowding and mean reversion: four premium platforms in one window compress the lead to 12 to 18 months; Demant's wholesale grew 17% organically in Q3 calendar 2026 against Sonova's 11%; Demant's next platform is due on its own cadence. Financial effect: share gains fade (above).
  • B2, retail returns fall as the network grows: each acquisition and each new patient costs more (customer acquisition USD 450 to 800 and rising; mid-teens margins; Redburn's ROIC-dilution warning). Effect: retail margin flat at best; group margin about -10 basis points a year from mix.
  • B3, channel conflict: Sonova is both a retailer and the supplier to independents (43% to 47% of its wholesale). Its Costco presence, its own clinics and Phonak's list-price rigidity irritate independents; at the same time independents resent GN for funding Amplifon. Displaced GN volume flows mainly to non-integrated makers (Starkey, WS Audiology) and only partly to Sonova. Net for the model: Amplifon supply loss of CHF 50m to 60m phased over FY2028 to FY2030, against a partial GN offset of perhaps CHF 10m to 20m a year (unconfirmed), so a net wholesale drag of about 0.5 to 0.7 points a year.
  • B4, deliberate reinvestment: management recycles 1 to 3 points of margin into R&D, IT and commercial capability; this caps margin expansion at about 35 basis points a year (P-01, P-03).
  • B5, buyback efficiency falls as the stock re-rates: at 21 times earnings each franc of buyback buys half the earnings it bought at 16 times in May 2026.

Net shape for the line items. Revenue: wholesale local-currency growth 9.5%, 5.5%, 4.6%, 5.0%, 5.5% (FY2027 to FY2031); retail about 7% (including 2 to 2.5 points of bolt-ons); Cochlear Implants -3%, then +8%, +6%, +5%, +5%. Margin: +120 basis points in FY2027, then +35 to 40 a year. Cash: free cash flow outruns high-return uses by CHF 300m to 400m a year, which shows up either as buybacks, a larger deal, or rising net cash.

Judgement: specific and built from stated building blocks, better than most peers: market growth, share gains, M&A, a margin bridge, FX at stated spot rates, restructuring excluded and quantified, and a flagged H2 investment step-up. It is silent on segment-level and customer-level drivers.

FX: translation impact stated at early-month spot rates (May and November 2026). Exclusions: restructuring and transaction costs of CHF 35m to 40m. Capital: leverage 1.0x to 1.5x, dividend 40% to 45% payout, CHF 80m to 100m bolt-ons, no buyback in FY2027.

Missing. Segment guidance (wholesale, retail, implants), the Amplifon effect (only "embedded"), Costco, VA, implant approval timing, tax rate, retail returns.

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is informal or qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus, and why?

Judgement: informal commentary is above formal guidance and broadly in line with or slightly above consensus; management talks with more confidence than its numbers, which is consistent with deliberately conservative formal guidance.

Above-guidance tone. EON conversion "considerably faster than management's baseline" (Jefferies); "surpassed its fair share" at the large US retailer (Costco) with continued benefit expected and no harm from a fifth competitor's entry; "double down in the custom category with more innovation"; "Don't expect our ambition to go down"; the CHF 6bn path "remains intact" (August 2026); "With the strategy and leadership in place, we are now focused on execution"; robust cash conversion gives "ample firing power"; Asia mid-teens growth highlighted; Amplifon's phase-out "embedded" in guidance and downplayed for FY2027; market growth assumption raised.

Below or cautious. Flagged the H2 investment step-up; cochlear recovery dependent on FDA timing "not under the company's direct control"; rising customer acquisition costs acknowledged; the CHF 6bn called an ambition, not a target.

Why. A new team that rebased expectations wants to beat; the formal numbers protect against a crowded launch window and H2 comparisons; the tone signals that the team expects the top end.

Implication. The modelling skew for FY2027 is to the upside of formal guidance; the bigger uncertainty is FY2028, where the tone is ambitious but the comparatives, Amplifon and the franc are tougher.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

D4623rd of 38

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: four things are uncommon and matter for modelling: guidance in constant currency with FX restated at spot each half-year; a recent change of headline metric; semi-annual reporting in an industry where the main peer reports quarterly; and a chief executive who refuses customer-level disclosure.

  1. Constant-currency guidance with spot FX. Core EBIT growth is guided in constant currency; the franc effect is given separately at early-month spot rates and changes each half (from -2 to -3 points in May 2026 to neutral in November 2026). Reported francs can therefore diverge sharply from guidance; the Consumer must model FX explicitly.
  2. Metric change. Core EBIT replaced normalised EBITA in March 2026 (including acquisition amortisation, excluding restructuring); historical series need restating; the reported-to-core gap persists.
  3. Semi-annual reporting. Results in November and May only; between prints the market trades on monthly VA data, peer quarterlies and conferences (EUHA, October). This raises event volatility around the two prints and makes the VA series disproportionately influential.
  4. Disclosure boundaries. No comment on Costco, large customers or channel margins; retail unit economics opaque.
  5. Other: the late-2025 withdrawal of mid-term guidance; a stable IR team; a long-term "ambition" (CHF 6bn) separate from targets; no tax-rate guidance.

Revisions (FactSet, FY2028 consensus, 19-Nov-26). EPS +1% (1 month), +6% (3 months), +9% (6 months), +5% (12 months); EBITDA -2%, +4%, +5%, +1%; sales 0%, +3%, +3%, -4% (12 months negative because of the March 2026 reclassification); target price +14%, +28%, +35%; buy share 17%, -10 points in a month and -5 over three. Signal. The "estimates cut deeply and guidance rebased" opportunity existed in May 2026 at about CHF 193 and 16x to 18x; it has been taken. Now the composite is "estimates rising, ratings falling": the near-term EBITDA revision turned negative (-2% over one month), the buy share is falling and targets are chasing the price, which is the profile of a re-rated stock where further upside depends on further upgrades (C-84).

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts for the next 18 months are predictable in their operating substance and only moderately predictable in what will be reported in francs, and the unpredictable part is concentrated in one variable and one reporting period.

The earlier sets are congruent, and the one material error in them, the core EBIT base, cuts in a useful direction: the FY2027 forecast now sits at the top of guidance and level with consensus rather than above both, and the margin it requires (22.3%) has already been earned over the last twelve months. FY2027 is therefore close to a known quantity: core EBIT of about CHF 864m and core EPS of about CHF 11.8, within 2% to 3% either way.

Beyond May 2027 the cone widens and leans down. FY2028 core EPS of about CHF 12.5 has a four-in-five range of CHF 11.6 to 13.2 and consensus (CHF 12.84) sits in its upper third. The width comes mostly from the franc, which carries about 40% of the whole difference between the forecasts and the last four years and which the business has enjoyed neutral in about one year in three; then from the launch window in independents, where there is no data. The adverse items cluster in the first half of FY2028: the hardest comparison, annualised channel wins, Amplifon, a normal financial result, the end of tax relief, the franc at its forecast peak and a change of presentation standard. On my base that half reports core EPS growth of about 2% and the half before it about nil, even though core EBIT grows 6% to 7% in each. The memo should present the 18 months as two periods: to May 2027, low risk to the numbers and little new information; May to November 2027, the test.

Three features help. The undemonstrated parts of the forecast (net savings, deployment, the implant recovery) matter little before FY2029. Monitoring between prints, though not supplied by the company, is better than for most medical-technology companies. And the share price and its multiple are driven mainly by Sonova's own results, so the analysis in these sets bears directly on the outcome. One feature hurts: the line from operating profit to EPS is noisy, and the market has recently rewarded an EPS growth rate (36%) of which nearly a third came from the financial result.

For the memo's topic on where the shares are in their life cycle, the evidence in this set points to a late reinforcing phase: delivery is at its peak, the multiple has moved ahead of earnings, and the next two prints will show growth slowing on the measures the market watches.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

B+642nd of 12

Judgement: FY2027 relies only on what has already been delivered; about three-quarters of the growth forecast for FY2028 to FY2031 rests on strengths Sonova has shown (in-cycle share gains, operating leverage in local currency, cost-programme delivery, cash generation), and about one-quarter on things it has not shown: savings that survive reinvestment, retail acquisitions that do not dilute, a deployed balance sheet and a lower franc cost share. Three points the memo Analyst should carry.

  1. The margin "step" in FY2027 is not a forecast; it is in the trailing numbers. The second half needs only to hold roughly the margin of a year earlier. This is the strongest single support for the 18-month case and it was hidden by the wrong base.
  2. The destination is not unprecedented. A core EBIT margin of about 23.7% by FY2031 is roughly where Sonova stood in FY2022 on FactSet's basis (23.3%) while it still carried the loss-making consumer business. The AlphaSense report calls the March 2026 margin bridge "historically rare execution", but my forecast uses less than half of that bridge.
  3. The undemonstrated quarter is back-end loaded. Net savings, buybacks and the implant recovery contribute little before FY2029. For the next 18 months the only undemonstrated requirement is the franc.

Past delivery against its own guidance is mixed (profit guidance missed in two of the six years before the new team: FY2023 and FY2024), which is why the plausibility is good rather than high.

Judgement: narrow and roughly symmetric to May 2027 (FY2027 core EPS of about CHF 11.8, with four chances in five of landing within about 2.5% either way); then moderately wide and negatively skewed (FY2028 core EPS of about CHF 12.5, four chances in five of CHF 11.6 to 13.2, that is -7.5% to +5.5%, with a left tail to about CHF 10.5 and a right tail to about CHF 13.9). Consensus for FY2028 (CHF 12.84) sits in the upper third of that range: I put the chance of FY2028 EPS reaching it at about one in three. The case is predictable in local-currency operations and only moderately predictable in reported francs.

FY2027 (reported 18 May 2027). The first half (CHF 5.37) is 45% of the year. The second half turns on revenue (plus or minus 2% is about CHF 16m of EBIT), the size of the investment step-up, the financial result (the difference between a normal half and a repeat of the first half is about CHF 0.15 a share) and Integro.
